  │                                                      │
  │  ALLOW RULES (explicit):                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432 (PostgreSQL)       │  │
  │  │ anihpj-api → anihpj-redis:6379 (Cache)         │  │
  │  │ anihpj-web → anihpj-api:8000 (HTTP)            │  │
  │  │ monitoring → anihpj-api:9090 (/metrics)        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                      │
  │  REQUIRES: CNI with NetworkPolicy support             │
  │  ✅ Calico ✅ Cilium ✅ Weave Net                     │
  │  ❌ Default flannel/kubenet (no NetworkPolicy)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: NetworkPolicies enforce least-privilege networking using Calico CNI. anihpj-api can ONLY reach anihpj-db (5432) and Redis (6379). All other egress is blocked. Options A, B, and D are wrong: Service enables but doesn't restrict (A), Ingress controls north-south HTTP only (B), and RBAC controls API access not network traffic (D).

Observability & Security Q87 of 200
In the anihpj Istio mesh, each pod gets a unique identity like spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj-prod/sa/anihpj-api. What is SPIFFE, and why is it important for zero-trust networking?
A) SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework for Everyone) is a CNCF standard for workload identity. It assigns each workload a cryptographically verifiable identity (SPIFFE ID) that is independent of network location (IP/port). In service meshes, SPIFFE IDs are embedded in X.509 certificates (SVIDs), enabling mTLS with identity-based authorization: "Allow SPIFFE ID anihpj-api to call anihpj-db" — this is zero-trust: identity, not network location, determines access
B) SPIFFE is a DNS-based service discovery protocol that replaces CoreDNS for resolving pod hostnames within the service mesh
C) SPIFFE is a container runtime interface (CRI) that provides hardware-level isolation between pods for security-sensitive workloads
D) SPIFFE is a Kubernetes admission controller that validates pod security contexts before scheduling
✅ Correct Answer: A

SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework for Everyone) is a CNCF graduated standard for workload identity in dynamic environments. Each workload receives a SPIFFE ID (URI like spiffe://cluster.local/ns/prod/sa/anihpj-api) that is independent of network location — the identity stays the same even when the pod moves to a different node or gets a new IP. This identity is embedded in an SVID (SPIFFE Verifiable Identity Document), typically an X.509 certificate. In zero-trust, authorization is based on identity, not IP: "Allow SPIFFE ID anihpj-api to call SPIFFE ID anihpj-db" works regardless of where either pod runs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SPIFFE — Identity That Survives Pod Restarts
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SPIFFE ID: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/prod/sa/anihpj-api │
  │                                                      │
  │  TRADITIONAL (IP-based):                             │
  │  Pod restarts → new IP 10.244.3.99 → firewall rule  │
  │  breaks → manual update needed → downtime            │
  │                                                      │
  │  SPIFFE (identity-based):                            │
  │  Pod restarts → new IP BUT same SPIFFE ID → same    │
  │  certificate → authorization rules still work ✓      │
  │                                                      │
  │  SVID: X.509 cert with SPIFFE ID embedded            │
  │  Istio/Linkerd issue SVIDs automatically at pod start│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  AuthorizationPolicy: Allow SPIFFE anihpj-api → SPIFFE anihpj-db

For anihpj: Istio issues SPIFFE IDs to all pods via the Istio CA. AuthorizationPolicy rules in Istio use SPIFFE identities — "Allow anihpj-web to call anihpj-api" — instead of IP addresses that change with every restart. Options B, C, and D misrepresent SPIFFE entirely: it's not DNS (B), not a container runtime (C), and not an admission controller (D).

Observability & Security Q88 of 200
The anihpj-api pod needs to call an external payment API at api.payments.com:443. The platform team must control which external services pods can reach. How is this enforced?
A) Ingress rules — Ingress resources define rules for routing external HTTP traffic into the cluster, not for controlling outbound traffic from pods
B) DNS policy in CoreDNS — configure CoreDNS to block resolution of certain external domains for specific namespaces
C) Firewall rules on the node — configure iptables rules on each worker node to block outbound traffic to specific IP ranges
D) NetworkPolicy with egress rules + ServiceEntry (Istio) — NetworkPolicy can restrict egress to specific IP blocks/ports; ServiceEntry in Istio registers external services in the mesh, enabling mTLS to external endpoints and fine-grained egress control. Combined, they provide defense-in-depth: NetworkPolicy at L3/L4 + mesh at L7
✅ Correct Answer: D

Egress control is critical for platform security — NetworkPolicy restricts L3/L4 egress (IP blocks, ports), while service mesh adds L7 control and TLS origination to external services. Combined, they provide defense-in-depth: NetworkPolicy at the network layer and the mesh at the application layer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Egress Control — Defense-in-Depth for Outbound Traffic
  NetworkPolicy: Allow egress to 203.0.113.0/24:443
  Istio ServiceEntry: Register api.payments.com in mesh
  Istio Egress Gateway: Route all external traffic through gateway
  Combined: L3/L4 firewall + L7 traffic control + mTLS to external

For anihpj: NetworkPolicy allows egress only to api.payments.com:443. Istio ServiceEntry enables mTLS to the external API. All other egress is blocked by default-deny. Options A, B, and C are incomplete — Ingress controls inbound traffic (not outbound), DNS policy only blocks name resolution (not direct IP connections), and node-level iptables rules don't follow pods across node failures.

Observability & Security Q89 of 200
External users access anihpj-web at https://jobs.anihpj.com. The platform team configures TLS termination at the Ingress. What is Ingress TLS, and where does encryption end?
A) Ingress TLS encrypts traffic from the Ingress controller all the way to the backend pod — the pod must implement its own TLS endpoint
B) Ingress TLS terminates (decrypts) HTTPS at the Ingress controller; traffic from the Ingress to backend pods is typically plain HTTP (within the cluster). The TLS Secret (certificate + private key) is stored in Kubernetes and referenced by the Ingress resource. For end-to-end encryption, combine Ingress TLS with mTLS inside the cluster (service mesh)
C) Ingress TLS is a Kubernetes feature that automatically generates certificates for every pod in the cluster using a built-in CA
D) Ingress TLS only encrypts traffic between the client's browser and the DNS resolver; the Ingress controller handles traffic in plain HTTP
✅ Correct Answer: B

Ingress TLS terminates (decrypts) HTTPS at the Ingress Controller (the cluster edge). The TLS certificate and private key are stored in a Kubernetes TLS Secret, referenced by the Ingress resource. After termination, traffic from the Ingress controller to backend pods is plain HTTP by default — this is a known gap. For defense-in-depth, combine Ingress TLS (north-south encryption) with service mesh mTLS (east-west encryption inside the cluster) to achieve true end-to-end encryption: Browser → Ingress (TLS) → mesh (mTLS) → Pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ingress TLS + Mesh mTLS — End-to-End Encryption
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EXTERNAL (Internet)                                 │
  │  Browser ──HTTPS (TLS 1.3)──▶ Ingress Controller    │
  │                              TLS terminates HERE     │
  │  INTERNAL (Cluster)                                  │
  │  Ingress ──HTTP (plain)──▶ anihpj-web pod            │
  │              ↑                                       │
  │              GAP: internal traffic is unencrypted!   │
  │                                                      │
  │  WITH SERVICE MESH (defense-in-depth):               │
  │  Browser ──HTTPS──▶ Ingress ──mTLS──▶ anihpj-web    │
  │                                          ──mTLS──▶ anihpj-api
  │  ✅ End-to-end encryption achieved                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  cert-manager auto-provisions TLS certs from Let's Encrypt

For anihpj: cert-manager provisions Let's Encrypt cert for jobs.anihpj.com → stored as TLS Secret → Ingress references it for edge termination. Istio mTLS encrypts all internal traffic. Options A, C, and D are wrong: TLS does not extend to the pod (A), cert-manager generates certs but K8s doesn't have a built-in CA for this (C), and DNS has nothing to do with transport encryption (D).

Observability & Security Q90 of 200
The anihpj platform stores database passwords, API keys, and TLS certificates in Kubernetes. What is the proper way to manage sensitive configuration data in Kubernetes?
A) ConfigMap — stores non-sensitive configuration data as key-value pairs; passwords stored in ConfigMaps are visible in plain text to anyone with ConfigMap read access
B) Environment variables hardcoded in the Deployment YAML — values are stored in Git and visible to anyone with repository access
C) Secrets — Kubernetes Secrets store sensitive data (base64-encoded). They can be mounted as files (volume mount, recommended) or injected as environment variables. Best practice: use External Secrets Operator to sync secrets from external vaults (HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault) rather than storing them directly in Kubernetes; enable encryption at rest for Secrets in etcd
D) Annotate the Deployment with sensitive values — annotations are stored in etcd and visible via kubectl describe, providing no security benefit over ConfigMaps
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kubernetes Secrets are the built-in mechanism for sensitive data, but they have critical limitations that platform engineers MUST address. Secrets are only base64-encoded (NOT encrypted by default — anyone with etcd access can decode them), and they have no rotation mechanism. The best-practice stack: use External Secrets Operator (ESO) to sync secrets from an external vault (HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault, GCP Secret Manager) into K8s Secrets; enable etcd encryption at rest (--encryption-provider-config); mount secrets as files (not environment variables — env vars leak via /proc, crash dumps, and child processes); and use tools like Sealed Secrets or SOPS to encrypt secrets in Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secrets Management — Defense-in-Depth for Sensitive Data
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VAULT (External Source of Truth)                    │
  │  HashiCorp Vault / AWS Secrets Manager / Azure KV   │
  │    ↓ External Secrets Operator syncs automatically   │
  │  KUBERNETES SECRET (base64-encoded)                  │
  │    ↓ etcd encryption at rest enabled                 │
  │  POD (mount as file: /etc/secrets/db-password)       │
  │                                                      │
  │  ❌ NEVER: hardcode in YAML, commit to Git, env vars│
  │  ✅ ALWAYS: external vault + ESO + etcd encryption   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Sealed Secrets: Encrypt Secret → safe to store in Git → decrypt in cluster

For anihpj: DB passwords stored in HashiCorp Vault → External Secrets Operator syncs to K8s Secrets → anihpj-api mounts as /etc/secrets/db-password (file mount, not env var). etcd encryption at rest is enabled. Options A, B, and D are wrong: ConfigMaps are for non-sensitive data (A), hardcoded env vars in Git are a security anti-pattern (B), and annotations are visible via kubectl describe with no security (D).

2.3 Policy Engines for Platform GovernanceQ91-Q98

Observability & Security Q91 of 200
The anihpj platform team wants to enforce that all container images come from registry.anihpj.com and that no pods run as root. Instead of begging developers to follow these rules, they want automated enforcement. What is this approach called?
A) Policy-as-Code — defining rules programmatically (in YAML/Rego) that are automatically enforced by a policy engine at admission time. This eliminates manual compliance checking: if a pod spec violates the policy, the admission controller rejects it before it's created. Popular engines: OPA/Gatekeeper, Kyverno. This is the platform engineering way — enforce, don't beg
B) Code Review — requiring a senior engineer to manually review every Deployment YAML before it is applied to the cluster
C) Documentation — writing a wiki page that lists security requirements and hoping developers read and follow it
D) RBAC — restricting who can create pods, which prevents unauthorized access but does not validate the content of allowed pod specs
✅ Correct Answer: A

Policy-as-Code is the platform engineering approach to governance: define rules in code (YAML/Rego) that a policy engine enforces automatically at admission time. This eliminates the manual compliance bottleneck — instead of a human gatekeeper reviewing every Deployment for "non-root user? right registry? resource limits set?", the policy engine does it instantly and consistently. The platform team defines "what good looks like" once; the engine enforces it N times. This is the shift from "begging developers to follow best practices" to "the platform guarantees best practices."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy-as-Code — Enforce, Don't Beg
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT Policy-as-Code (Manual):                     │
  │  Dev writes YAML → Senior reviews → "Please fix X"   │
  │  → Dev fixes → Re-review → Apply → ❌ still may miss │
  │                                                      │
  │  WITH Policy-as-Code (Automated):                    │
  │  Dev writes YAML → kubectl apply → Admission Webhook │
  │  → Policy Engine → ❌ REJECTED: "Image must be from  │
  │  registry.anihpj.com" → Dev fixes → kubectl apply    │
  │  → ✅ ALLOWED (policy satisfied)                     │
  │                                                      │
  │  RESULT: Zero human gatekeeping, instant feedback,   │
  │  100% consistent enforcement for every single deploy  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Kyverno policies enforce image registry, non-root users, resource limits, and required labels. Every violation is rejected at admission time — before the pod exists. Options B (code review), C (documentation), and D (RBAC) are all human-reliant or incomplete approaches that don't scale.

Observability & Security Q92 of 200
The anihpj platform evaluates two policy engines: OPA/Gatekeeper and Kyverno. What are the key differences between them?
A) OPA/Gatekeeper uses Python for policy rules; Kyverno uses Go templates for policy definitions
B) OPA/Gatekeeper only enforces policies at runtime (after pods are running); Kyverno only enforces at build time (in CI pipelines)
C) OPA/Gatekeeper is a commercial product requiring a license; Kyverno is the only open-source policy engine for Kubernetes
D) OPA/Gatekeeper uses Rego (a declarative policy language with a learning curve but extreme flexibility); Kyverno uses familiar Kubernetes-style YAML (easier to learn, less flexible). Both are CNCF projects. Gatekeeper is built on OPA (general-purpose policy engine); Kyverno is Kubernetes-native (policies are K8s CRDs). Choose Kyverno for simplicity and K8s-native feel; choose Gatekeeper for complex cross-resource logic and multi-cluster policy distribution
✅ Correct Answer: D

The two leading CNCF policy engines serve different audiences: Kyverno uses Kubernetes-native YAML — policies ARE K8s custom resources with the same structure developers already know. Gatekeeper uses Rego — a powerful declarative language with a learning curve but extreme flexibility for complex cross-resource logic. Kyverno's mantra: "If you know K8s YAML, you know Kyverno." Gatekeeper's: "Any policy you can imagine, Rego can express." The platform engineering choice: Kyverno for simpler adoption and lower cognitive load; Gatekeeper for complex multi-cluster governance scenarios.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno vs Gatekeeper — Policy Engine Design Philosophy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KYVERNO (CNCF Incubating)                           │
  │  Language: Kubernetes YAML (familiar, intuitive)     │
  │  Policy: CRDs (ClusterPolicy, Policy)                │
  │  "Require non-root" = 10 lines of YAML               │
  │  Best for: Simplicity, team adoption, K8s-native feel│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  GATEKEEPER (CNCF Incubating, based on OPA)          │
  │  Language: Rego (powerful, declarative, steep)       │
  │  Policy: ConstraintTemplate + Constraint             │
  │  Best for: Complex cross-resource logic, multi-cluster│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  BOTH: Mutate + Validate + Generate + VerifyImages   │
  │  BOTH: Admission webhook integration                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Kyverno is chosen because the platform team values simplicity and fast adoption. "Require non-root" is a 10-line Kyverno ClusterPolicy that any developer can read and understand. Options A, B, and C misrepresent both tools: neither uses Python (A), both enforce at admission time (B), and both are fully open-source (C).

Observability & Security Q93 of 200
The anihpj platform uses Kyverno. They need a policy that automatically adds a runAsNonRoot: true security context to every pod. What type of Kyverno rule is this?
A) Validate rule — checks if the pod spec complies and rejects non-compliant resources; it cannot modify the resource
B) Mutate rule — modifies the resource during admission to add defaults or enforce settings. In Kyverno, mutate rules can add, replace, or remove fields. This is powerful for platform engineering: developers don't need to know about runAsNonRoot — the platform adds it automatically via mutation
C) Generate rule — creates new Kubernetes resources (like a default NetworkPolicy) when certain triggers occur
D) VerifyImages rule — checks container image signatures against Cosign to ensure only signed images are deployed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno Mutate rules are the platform engineering superpower: they automatically modify resources during admission to inject best practices WITHOUT requiring developers to know or specify them. While Validate rules act as gatekeepers ("You forgot runAsNonRoot — REJECTED!"), Mutate rules act as helpful defaults ("I see you didn't set runAsNonRoot — I'll add it for you ✓"). Mutation reduces developer cognitive load: the platform handles security defaults, required labels, sidecar injection — developers focus on business logic. Kyverno supports add, replace, and remove mutations on any field.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Rule Types — Validate, Mutate, Generate, VerifyImages
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VALIDATE: Check → Pass or Reject                    │
  │  "Image must be from registry.anihpj.com"           │
  │  Dev specifies docker.io/nginx → ❌ REJECTED         │
  │                                                      │
  │  MUTATE: Modify → Inject defaults silently           │
  │  "Add runAsNonRoot: true if not specified"           │
  │  Dev forgets securityContext → ✓ Platform adds it    │
  │                                                      │
  │  GENERATE: Create → Trigger companion resources      │
  │  "Every new namespace gets a default NetworkPolicy"  │
  │                                                      │
  │  VERIFY IMAGES: Sign → Check Cosign signatures       │
  │  "Only signed images from trusted publishers"        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Kyverno mutate rules inject runAsNonRoot: true, readOnlyRootFilesystem: true, drop ALL capabilities, and add required labels — developers submit simple pod specs and the platform enriches them. Options A (validate), C (generate), and D (verifyImages) describe other rule types — only mutate modifies resources.

Observability & Security Q94 of 200
When kubectl apply is run, the API Server validates the request and may reject it. What are admission controllers, and how do policy engines use them?
A) Admission controllers are kubectl plugins that run on the developer's machine before the YAML is sent to the API server
B) Admission controllers are Kubernetes controllers that watch for new pods and modify them after they are already running (post-start hooks)
C) Admission controllers are plugins that intercept requests to the API Server AFTER authentication/authorization but BEFORE the object is persisted to etcd. They can mutate (MutatingAdmissionWebhook) or validate (ValidatingAdmissionWebhook) the object. Policy engines like Kyverno and Gatekeeper register as admission webhooks — the API Server calls them on every resource create/update, and they return "allow" or "deny"
D) Admission controllers are a feature of the kubelet that validates container runtime configurations before starting pods on a node
✅ Correct Answer: C

Admission controllers are the final gatekeepers of the Kubernetes API Server — they intercept requests AFTER authentication and authorization but BEFORE the object is persisted to etcd. This is the critical design point: the user has proven who they are (authn) and that they have permission (RBAC), but the admission phase asks: "Is this REQUEST compliant with platform policy?" There are two types: MutatingAdmissionWebhook (can modify the object before persistence) and ValidatingAdmissionWebhook (can only approve or deny). Policy engines register as webhooks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Admission Control — The API Server's Last Line of Defense
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                           │
  │    ↓                                                  │
  │  AUTHENTICATION: "Who are you?" (cert/token/OIDC)    │
  │    ↓ User: jane@anihpj.com (verified)                 │
  │  AUTHORIZATION (RBAC): "Can you create pods?"         │
  │    ↓ Role deployer allows create pods ✓               │
  │  ADMISSION: "Is this pod compliant with policy?"     │
  │    ↓ Mutating Webhooks (Kyverno: add runAsNonRoot)   │
  │    ↓ Validating Webhooks (OPA: check image registry) │
  │    ↓ ✅ ALLOW or ❌ DENY with clear error            │
  │  etcd: Object persisted (only if allowed)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Built-in controllers: NamespaceLifecycle, LimitRanger, ResourceQuota, PSA

The admission phase is WHY policy engines work — they hook into the request pipeline at the exact point where decisions can be made before state changes. Options A, B, and D describe things that happen elsewhere: client-side (A), post-start (B), or kubelet-level (D).

Observability & Security Q95 of 200
The anihpj platform uses Pod Security Standards (PSS) to enforce pod security at the namespace level. What are the three PSS levels, and what do they enforce?
A) Privileged (unrestricted — for system workloads like kube-system), Baseline (prevents known privilege escalations — blocks hostPath, hostNetwork, privileged containers), and Restricted (hardens pods following best practices — requires runAsNonRoot, seccomp, drops all capabilities). Enforced via Pod Security Admission (PSA) controller at the namespace level with labels like pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
B) Development (allows everything), Staging (warns on violations), and Production (blocks all violations) — three operational environments for pod security
C) RBAC-based (uses roles to define pod security), Network-based (uses NetworkPolicy), and Storage-based (uses storage classes) — three security domains in Kubernetes
D) Low (only checks image registry), Medium (adds non-root check), and High (adds SELinux and AppArmor profiles) — three levels of image security scanning
✅ Correct Answer: A

Pod Security Standards (PSS) define three progressive security tiers that replace the deprecated PodSecurityPolicy (PSP). Privileged: unrestricted — for system workloads (kube-system, monitoring agents, CNI plugins). Baseline: prevents known privilege escalations — blocks hostPath volumes, hostNetwork, hostPID, privileged containers; suitable for most application workloads. Restricted: full hardening following Pod Hardening best practices — requires runAsNonRoot, seccomp profiles, drops ALL capabilities, read-only root filesystem; suitable for high-security workloads (payment processing, PII data). Enforcement is via Pod Security Admission (PSA), a built-in admission controller configured per-namespace with labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Security Standards — Three Tiers of Pod Hardening
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PRIVILEGED: Unrestricted                             │
  │  For: kube-system, monitoring agents, CNI plugins     │
  │  Allowed: hostPath, hostNetwork, privileged containers│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  BASELINE: Prevents known privilege escalations       │
  │  For: Most application workloads                      │
  │  Blocked: hostPath, hostNetwork, hostPID, privileged  │
  │  Allowed: Everything else                             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  RESTRICTED: Full Pod Hardening best practices        │
  │  For: Payment processing, PII data, sensitive workloads│
  │  Required: runAsNonRoot, seccomp, drop ALL caps,      │
  │            readOnlyRootFilesystem, non-root groups    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Namespace labels:
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted (BLOCKS)
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted (WARNS user)
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted (LOGS to audit)

For anihpj: anihpj-prod namespace is labeled enforce=restricted. anihpj-dev is enforce=baseline with warn=restricted — developers see warnings and prepare for production hardening before they deploy to prod. Options B, C, and D are wrong: they describe environments (B), unrelated security domains (C), and imaginary levels (D).

Observability & Security Q96 of 200
The anihpj platform uses Kyverno for policy enforcement. They want to see which resources in the cluster violate which policies — including resources that existed before the policy was created. How is this done?
A) Re-apply all resources with kubectl apply — existing resources are re-validated against current policies during each apply operation
B) Delete and recreate all pods — forcing recreation triggers admission webhooks, which validate against current policies
C) Check the Kyverno audit logs — every policy violation is automatically logged to the Kyverno pod's stdout and can be queried with Loki
D) Policy Reports — Kyverno continuously scans existing resources and generates PolicyReport CRDs (per-namespace) and ClusterPolicyReport CRDs (cluster-scoped) that list pass/fail/warn results for every resource against every policy. This gives platform teams a compliance dashboard showing exactly which resources violate which rules, without touching running workloads. ArgoCD and Backstage can surface these reports in their UI
✅ Correct Answer: D

Policy Reports are a game-changing Kyverno feature that provides continuous compliance visibility for ALL resources — including those that existed BEFORE a policy was created. Kyverno continuously scans the cluster and generates PolicyReport CRDs (per-namespace) and ClusterPolicyReport CRDs (cluster-scoped) listing pass/fail/warn results for every resource against every policy. This enables: (1) auditing existing resources without recreating them, (2) measuring policy rollout progress, (3) surfacing violations in Backstage/ArgoCD, and (4) incremental policy adoption — warn for weeks, review reports, then enforce.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy Reports — Continuous Compliance Without Recreating Resources
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno Background Controller                       │
  │    ↓ continuous scanning every N minutes              │
  │  Evaluates ALL resources against ALL policies         │
  │    ↓                                                  │
  │  PolicyReport CRD (per namespace):                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-api-7d8f9:                               │  │
  │  │   require-non-root: PASS ✓                      │  │
  │  │   require-resource-limits: FAIL ✗               │  │
  │  │   require-image-registry: WARN ⚠                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                      │
  │  SURFACED IN: Backstage, ArgoCD, Grafana dashboards  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  POLICY ROLLOUT STRATEGY:
  Week 1-2: audit mode → PolicyReports show violations
  Week 3: enforce mode → violations blocked

For anihpj: Policy reports are surfaced in Backstage on each service's page. A developer sees "Your anihpj-api pods are failing require-resource-limits" with a link to the exact policy and fix instructions — all before enforcement kicks in. Options A, B, and C are wrong: re-applying (A) and deleting pods (B) are disruptive, and Kyverno logs (C) don't provide structured per-resource reports.

Observability & Security Q97 of 200
A developer submits a pod with privileged: true to the anihpj-prod namespace (labeled enforce=restricted). What happens when kubectl apply is run?
A) The pod is created but marked with a warning annotation — it runs with privileged access until the security team manually deletes it
B) The pod is REJECTED by the Pod Security Admission controller before it reaches etcd. The developer receives a clear error message: "pods 'privileged-pod' is forbidden: violates PodSecurity 'restricted:latest': privileged (container must not set securityContext.privileged=true)". The pod never exists in the cluster — enforcement happens at admission time, not runtime
C) The pod is created but immediately terminated by the kubelet when it detects the privileged security context at container start time
D) The pod is created successfully because privileged: true is a valid Kubernetes field — Pod Security Admission only checks RBAC permissions
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pod Security Admission (PSA) enforces at admission time — BEFORE the object is persisted to etcd and BEFORE the pod is scheduled. When a pod with privileged: true is submitted to a namespace labeled enforce=restricted, PSA checks the pod spec, finds the violation, and REJECTS with a clear error message. The pod NEVER exists in the cluster — not in etcd, not on any node, nowhere. This is defense-in-depth at the API level: the developer gets immediate feedback with the exact violation and how to fix it, rather than discovering the issue hours later when the pod is mysteriously terminated.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA Enforcement — Rejected at Admission, Before Pod Exists
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f privileged-pod.yaml                │
  │    ↓                                                  │
  │  API Server receives request                         │
  │    ↓ Authentication: jane@anihpj.com ✓               │
  │    ↓ Authorization (RBAC): create pods ✓              │
  │    ↓ Admission: PSA checks namespace label            │
  │          anihpj-prod: enforce=restricted              │
  │    ↓ PSA evaluates pod spec:                          │
  │          securityContext.privileged=true              │
  │    ↓ ❌ DENIED                                        │
  │                                                      │
  │  Error returned to kubectl:                           │
  │  "pods 'privileged-pod' is forbidden: violates        │
  │   PodSecurity 'restricted:latest': privileged         │
  │   (container must not set securityContext             │
  │   .privileged=true)"                                  │
  │                                                      │
  │  Pod NEVER created. No cleanup needed.                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: anihpj-prod enforces restricted. Developers get fast, clear feedback when their pod spec violates standards — they fix it and re-apply. Options A, C, and D are wrong: the pod is NOT created with a warning (A), NOT created then terminated (C), and NOT created successfully (D). PSA blocks at admission, period.

Observability & Security Q98 of 200
The anihpj platform team wants a phased rollout of a new policy: warn for 2 weeks, then enforce. How do Kyverno and PSA support this pattern?
A) Manual enforcement — the platform team sends email warnings to developers for 2 weeks, then manually enables enforcement in the policy engine
B) Canary policy deployment — the policy is first applied to 10% of pods, gradually increasing to 100% over 2 weeks
C) Audit/Warn mode → Enforce mode — Kyverno policies can be set to "audit" (report violations in PolicyReport but don't block) or "enforce" (block violations). PSA supports namespace labels: pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn (shows warning to user), pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit (logs to audit log), and pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce (blocks). The platform team sets warn+audit for 2 weeks, reviews PolicyReports to identify violations, works with teams to fix them, then switches to enforce
D) A/B testing — two identical namespaces, one with policy enabled and one without, to compare outcomes
✅ Correct Answer: C

Gradual policy rollout is the social contract of platform engineering: don't surprise developers with blocking policies. Both Kyverno and PSA support a 3-phase rollout: (1) Audit/Warn — violations are logged to PolicyReports and shown as warnings to users, but NOT blocked. (2) Teams review reports, fix violations at their own pace. (3) Enforce — violations are blocked. This builds trust: the platform helps developers see what needs to change before it becomes a hard requirement. PSA enables this with three namespace labels: warn, audit, and enforce — each can target a different PSS level.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy Rollout — Audit → Warn → Enforce
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PHASE 1: AUDIT (Week 1-2)                            │
  │  Policies in audit mode → PolicyReports show violations│
  │  Pods still deploy, but violations are visible         │
  │  Teams see: "15 pods violating require-resource-limits"│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  PHASE 2: WARN (Week 2-3, overlapping)                │
  │  kubectl apply shows: "Warning: violates restricted"  │
  │  Pods still deploy, but developers get immediate signal│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  PHASE 3: ENFORCE (Week 4+)                           │
  │  Violations BLOCKED at admission                      │
  │  All teams have already fixed their violations         │
  │  Zero surprises, zero blocked deploys, zero friction   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PSA labels: warn=restricted, audit=restricted, enforce=restricted

For anihpj: New "require resource limits" policy started in audit mode for 2 weeks. Backstage showed violations per service. After teams reached 100% compliance, the policy was switched to enforce. Options A (manual emails), B (canary % of pods), and D (A/B testing) are not how policy engines support phased rollout.

2.4 Kubernetes Security EssentialsQ99-Q106

Observability & Security Q99 of 200
The anihpj platform team is designing their security architecture. They hear about the "4 C's of Cloud Native Security." What are they, and what do they cover?
A) Cloud (infrastructure: VPC, IAM, firewall, encryption at rest), Cluster (Kubernetes components: API Server security, etcd encryption, RBAC, NetworkPolicy), Container (image scanning, non-root user, read-only filesystem, resource limits), and Code (application security: static analysis, dependency scanning, secrets management, input validation). Each layer assumes the layer below it may be compromised — defense in depth across all four layers
B) Confidentiality, Integrity, Availability, and Compliance — the four pillars of information security applied to Kubernetes
C) Code, Compile, Containerize, and Cloud — the four stages of the software supply chain that must be secured
D) Create, Configure, Control, and Comply — the four phases of Kubernetes security lifecycle management
✅ Correct Answer: A

The 4 C's of Cloud Native Security is a defense-in-depth model from the Kubernetes documentation that organizes security into four concentric layers. Each layer assumes the layers below it may be compromised and provides its own security controls. Cloud (outermost): infrastructure security — IAM, VPC, firewall, KMS/encryption at rest, audit logging. Cluster: Kubernetes component security — API Server hardening, etcd encryption, RBAC, NetworkPolicy, PSA, mTLS. Container: image security — vulnerability scanning, non-root user, read-only filesystem, resource limits, signed images. Code (innermost): application security — SAST, dependency scanning, secrets in vaults (not code), input validation, secure defaults.

📝 Detailed Explanation
4 C's of Cloud Native Security — Concentric Defense-in-Depth
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    ☁️ CLOUD                           │
  │  IAM, VPC, KMS encryption, firewall, audit logging   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │              ⚙️ CLUSTER                         │  │
  │  │  RBAC, NetworkPolicy, etcd encryption, PSA,    │  │
  │  │  mTLS, API Server hardening, node security     │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │  │  │           📦 CONTAINER                    │  │  │
  │  │  │  Image scanning, non-root, read-only FS, │  │  │
  │  │  │  resource limits, signed images (Cosign) │  │  │
  │  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │        💻 CODE                     │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  SAST, SCA, secrets in vaults,     │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  input validation, secure defaults │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT: Each layer assumes lower layers may be breached.
  Compromised container? NetworkPolicy + non-root limit blast radius.

For anihpj: Every layer is secured independently. Code is SAST-scanned in CI. Containers run as non-root with read-only filesystems. Cluster enforces RBAC + NetworkPolicy + PSA restricted. Cloud layer uses IAM roles with least privilege and KMS for etcd encryption. Options B, C, and D are not the official Kubernetes security model.

Observability & Security Q100 of 200
The anihpj platform team needs to control who can create Deployments, who can read Secrets, and who can delete namespaces. What Kubernetes mechanism provides this fine-grained access control?
A) NetworkPolicy — controls network traffic between pods based on labels and ports, not API access to Kubernetes resources
B) Pod Security Admission — controls pod security contexts at the namespace level, not who can perform API operations
C) ServiceAccount — provides an identity for pods to authenticate to the API server, but does not define what operations are allowed
D) RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) — defines who (Subject: User/Group/ServiceAccount) can do what (Verb: get, list, create, update, delete, watch) on which resources (Deployments, Secrets, Namespaces) in which scope (ClusterRole = cluster-wide, Role = namespace-scoped). Authorization is the 'A' in AAA (Authentication, Authorization, Admission)
✅ Correct Answer: D

RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) is the authorization layer of Kubernetes — the "AuthZ" in the AAA model (Authentication, Authorization, Admission). RBAC answers: "Now that we know WHO you are, WHAT are you allowed to do?" The model has four components: Subject (User, Group, or ServiceAccount — WHO), Verb (get, list, create, update, delete, watch — WHAT operation), Resource (Deployments, Secrets, Namespaces — ON WHAT), and Scope (Role+RoleBinding = namespace-scoped; ClusterRole+ClusterRoleBinding = cluster-scoped). Principle of least privilege: grant only the verbs and resources each subject actually needs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes AAA — Authentication → Authorization → Admission
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AUTHENTICATION (AuthN): "Who are you?"               │
  │  Certificates, Bearer Tokens, OIDC, ServiceAccounts   │
  │    ↓ User: jane@anihpj.com (verified)                 │
  │                                                      │
  │  AUTHORIZATION (AuthZ — RBAC): "What can you do?"     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Role: deployer (namespace: anihpj-prod)         │  │
  │  │   apiGroups: ["apps", ""]                       │  │
  │  │   resources: ["deployments", "configmaps"]      │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "create", "update"]    │  │
  │  │ RoleBinding: jane → deployer                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │    ↓ Jane can create Deployments ✓                   │
  │                                                      │
  │  ADMISSION: "Is this request compliant?"             │
  │    ↓ Kyverno, PSA, OPA/Gatekeeper                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ClusterRole: Cluster-wide scope (nodes, PVs, namespaces)
  Role: Single namespace scope (deployments, configmaps)

For anihpj: Developers have Role "deployer" (create/get/update Deployments and ConfigMaps in their namespace). CI/CD ServiceAccount has Role "ci-deployer" (create/update all workload resources). Secrets are restricted to platform team only via a separate Role. Options A, B, and C control different things: NetworkPolicy controls network (A), PSA controls pod security contexts (B), and ServiceAccount provides identity but not permissions (C).

Observability & Security Q101 of 200
The anihpj platform team has two needs: (1) Let the anihpj-dev team create Deployments and ConfigMaps inside only the anihpj-dev namespace, and (2) Let the platform-admin team view all nodes across the entire cluster. Which RBAC resource combination correctly handles BOTH use cases?
A) A single Role with rules for deployments and nodes, bound to both teams via one RoleBinding — a Role can contain any resources including cluster-scoped ones like nodes
B) Two ClusterRoles (one for deployments, one for nodes), both bound via ClusterRoleBindings — ClusterRoleBinding is the only way to grant any Kubernetes permissions
C) A Role (namespace-scoped: deployments, configmaps in anihpj-dev) + RoleBinding for the dev team, and a ClusterRole (cluster-scoped: get/list/watch nodes) + ClusterRoleBinding for the admin team. Role/RoleBinding = single namespace. ClusterRole/ClusterRoleBinding = entire cluster
D) Two Namespace-restricted ClusterRoles with namespaceSelector set to "anihpj-dev", plus ClusterRoleBindings — ClusterRoles can be scoped to a namespace by adding a namespaceSelector field
✅ Correct Answer: C

Role + RoleBinding = namespace-scoped. ClusterRole + ClusterRoleBinding = cluster-scoped. This is the critical distinction in Kubernetes RBAC. A Role can only grant access to resources within a single namespace. If you need to grant access to cluster-scoped resources (like Nodes, PersistentVolumes, Namespaces themselves), you MUST use a ClusterRole. A ClusterRole can also be used with a RoleBinding (not ClusterRoleBinding) to grant namespace-scoped access to a ClusterRole's rules — useful when you want to reuse the same set of permissions across multiple namespaces without duplicating Roles.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Resource Types — Role vs ClusterRole Decision Tree
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     RBAC RESOURCE DECISION                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Q: Is the resource you're granting NAMESPACED?                  │
  │     (pods, deployments, services, configmaps, secrets, etc.)     │
  │                                                                 │
  │     YES ──▶ Do you need access in ONE namespace only?            │
  │             YES ──▶ Role + RoleBinding ✓                         │
  │             NO (multiple ns) ──▶ ClusterRole + RoleBinding ✓     │
  │                                                                 │
  │     NO (cluster-scoped: nodes, PVs, namespaces, etc.)           │
  │         ──▶ ClusterRole + ClusterRoleBinding ✓                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  ROLE                                 CLUSTERROLE         │    │
  │  │  ├─ Namespace-scoped                   ├─ Cluster-scoped   │    │
  │  │  ├─ Bound via RoleBinding              ├─ Bound via:       │    │
  │  │  ├─ Cannot grant cluster resources     │  │ ClusterRoleBinding → cluster-wide  │
  │  │  └─ Example: deployer in anihpj-dev    │  │ RoleBinding → namespace-scoped     │
  │  │                                        │  └─ Example: node-reader (all nodes)  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  anihpj RBAC DESIGN:                                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Dev Team (anihpj-dev ns only)           Admin Team (all nodes)  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Role: anihpj-dev-deploy │            │ ClusterRole:         │ │
  │  │  apiGroups: ["apps"]    │            │  node-reader          │ │
  │  │  resources: ["deploy",  │            │   apiGroups: [""]     │ │
  │  │    "configmaps"]        │            │   resources: ["nodes"]│ │
  │  │  verbs: ["get","list",  │            │   verbs: ["get",      │ │
  │  │    "create","update"]   │            │     "list","watch"]   │ │
  │  │                          │            │                       │ │
  │  │ RoleBinding: dev→deploy │            │ ClusterRoleBinding:   │ │
  │  │  namespace: anihpj-dev  │            │  admin→node-reader    │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The dev team's Role is scoped to anihpj-dev namespace only — they cannot accidentally modify resources in anihpj-prod. The admin team's ClusterRole lets them monitor all nodes for capacity planning. Option A is wrong because a Role cannot grant access to cluster-scoped resources like nodes. Option B is wrong because RoleBinding (not ClusterRoleBinding) can bind a ClusterRole to a namespace. Option D is wrong because ClusterRoles do not have a namespaceSelector field — they are inherently cluster-wide when bound with ClusterRoleBinding.

Observability & Security Q102 of 200
The anihpj-api pod needs to call the Kubernetes API to list ConfigMaps in its own namespace. How does the pod authenticate to the API server, and what security best practice should the platform team apply?
A) A ServiceAccount provides pod identity — a signed JWT token is automatically mounted at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token inside the pod. Best practice: create a dedicated ServiceAccount (anihpj-api-sa) with only the RBAC permissions the pod needs, and set automountServiceAccountToken: false on pods that never call the API server
B) Pods authenticate using the kubeconfig file stored in the container image — each pod image contains a kubeconfig with a client certificate signed by the cluster CA. Best practice: rotate the certificate in the image every 90 days
C) Pods authenticate using OIDC — each pod container runs an OIDC sidecar that obtains an ID token from the platform's identity provider (like Google Workspace or Azure AD). Best practice: use short-lived tokens with 5-minute expiry
D) Pods authenticate using the node's kubelet credentials — since every pod runs on a node, it inherits the kubelet's client certificate. Best practice: restrict the node's RBAC permissions to limit what pods can do
✅ Correct Answer: A

ServiceAccounts are the identity mechanism for pods in Kubernetes. Every namespace has a default ServiceAccount, but best practice is to create dedicated ServiceAccounts with scoped RBAC permissions. When a pod is created, the ServiceAccount's JWT token is automatically mounted into the pod at a well-known path. The pod's application code reads this token and presents it to the API server as a Bearer token. The API server validates it, extracts the ServiceAccount username (system:serviceaccount:<namespace>:<name>), and passes it through RBAC authorization.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceAccount Authentication — Pod → API Server Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    SERVICEACCOUNT AUTH FLOW                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  1. CREATE                                                      │
  │  kubectl create sa anihpj-api-sa -n anihpj-prod                 │
  │    ↓ Creates SA + auto-creates a Secret containing a JWT token  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  2. ASSIGN RBAC                                                 │
  │  kubectl create role configmap-reader --verb=get,list,watch     │
  │    --resource=configmaps -n anihpj-prod                         │
  │  kubectl create rolebinding api-cm --role=configmap-reader      │
  │    --serviceaccount=anihpj-prod:anihpj-api-sa -n anihpj-prod    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3. POD USES SA                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Pod: anihpj-api-7d8f9 (namespace: anihpj-prod)          │   │
  │  │  serviceAccountName: anihpj-api-sa                        │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │  Token auto-mounted at:                                   │   │
  │  │  /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/           │   │
  │  │    ├── token          ← JWT (signed by API server)       │   │
  │  │    ├── ca.crt         ← Cluster CA certificate           │   │
  │  │    └── namespace      ← "anihpj-prod"                    │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  4. AUTHENTICATION                                              │
  │  anihpj-api code reads token → HTTP request to API server:      │
  │  GET /api/v1/namespaces/anihpj-prod/configmaps                  │
  │  Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOi...<JWT>                       │
  │    ↓ API server validates JWT signature                         │
  │    ↓ Extracts: system:serviceaccount:anihpj-prod:anihpj-api-sa  │
  │    ↓ RBAC check: anihpj-api-sa has Role "configmap-reader" ✓   │
  │    ↓ Returns ConfigMap list                                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEST PRACTICES:                                                │
  │  ✅ Dedicated SA per pod (never use "default" SA)              │
  │  ✅ Least privilege: grant only required verbs + resources      │
  │  ✅ automountServiceAccountToken: false if pod doesn't call API │
  │  ✅ Bound tokens (projected, time-limited, audience-bound)      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The anihpj-api pod uses ServiceAccount anihpj-api-sa which has RBAC to read ConfigMaps (for app configuration) and update its own leader-election ConfigMap. The anihpj-web pod never calls the API server, so it has automountServiceAccountToken: false — no token is mounted, reducing the attack surface. Option B is wrong because container images should never contain kubeconfig files or certificates — that would be a security disaster. Option C is wrong because OIDC is for human users, not pods (though cloud IAM roles for pods via IRSA/EKS Pod Identity use a similar concept with webhook token exchange). Option D is wrong because pods do NOT inherit kubelet credentials — that would break all RBAC isolation between namespaces.

Observability & Security Q103 of 200
The anihpj platform team wants to harden the anihpj-api container. They need it to: (1) run as a non-root user, (2) have a read-only root filesystem, (3) drop all Linux capabilities, and (4) prevent privilege escalation. Which Kubernetes mechanism provides ALL of these controls in a single Pod spec field?
A) NetworkPolicy — controls network-level ingress/egress rules between pods using label selectors and port ranges; does not govern OS-level container behavior
B) ResourceQuota — limits aggregate resource consumption (CPU, memory, storage, object count) per namespace; does not control container runtime behavior
C) PodDisruptionBudget — ensures a minimum number of pods remain available during voluntary disruptions like node drains or cluster upgrades; unrelated to container security hardening
D) securityContext — a Pod-level and container-level spec field that configures: runAsNonRoot (reject containers that run as UID 0), runAsUser: 1000 (force a specific non-root UID), readOnlyRootFilesystem: true (prevent writes to the container's root filesystem), capabilities.drop: ["ALL"] (remove all Linux capabilities), and allowPrivilegeEscalation: false (prevent child processes from gaining more privileges than their parent). Can be set at both Pod-level and per-container, with container-level overriding Pod-level
✅ Correct Answer: D

The securityContext is the single most important field for pod and container hardening. It can be set at two levels: Pod-level (applies to all containers in the pod as defaults) and container-level (overrides Pod-level for a specific container). The key fields every platform engineer must know: runAsNonRoot: true (container will NOT start if it tries to run as root/UID 0 — catches misconfigured images), runAsUser + runAsGroup (force specific UID/GID), fsGroup (group ownership of mounted volumes), readOnlyRootFilesystem (immutable container filesystem), capabilities.drop (remove dangerous Linux capabilities like CAP_SYS_ADMIN), allowPrivilegeEscalation: false (prevent setuid binaries and child privilege gain), and seccompProfile (system call filtering).

📝 Detailed Explanation
securityContext — Pod-Level vs Container-Level Hardening
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        securityContext — DEFENSE-IN-DEPTH AT THE POD LEVEL       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Pod-Level securityContext (applies as defaults to all containers)│
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ securityContext:                                         │    │
  │  │   runAsNonRoot: true          ← Block any root (UID 0)   │    │
  │  │   runAsUser: 1000             ← Force specific UID       │    │
  │  │   runAsGroup: 3000            ← Force specific GID       │    │
  │  │   fsGroup: 2000               ← Volume file ownership    │    │
  │  │   seccompProfile:                                         │    │
  │  │     type: RuntimeDefault      ← System call filter        │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Container-Level (overrides/extends Pod-level)                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ containers:                                               │    │
  │  │ - name: anihpj-api                                       │    │
  │  │   securityContext:                                        │    │
  │  │     allowPrivilegeEscalation: false  ← Block setuid bins  │    │
  │  │     readOnlyRootFilesystem: true      ← Immutable FS      │    │
  │  │     capabilities:                                          │    │
  │  │       drop: ["ALL"]                 ← Remove ALL caps     │    │
  │  │       add: ["NET_BIND_SERVICE"]     ← Only bind to port  │    │
  │  │     privileged: false               ← No privileged mode  │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FIELD REFERENCE — What Each One Prevents:                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ runAsNonRoot: true │ Container with USER root or UID 0    │  │
  │  │                    │ in Dockerfile → Pod will NOT start   │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ runAsUser: 1000    │ Overrides the UID in the container   │  │
  │  │                    │ image — even if image says root      │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ readOnlyRootFS     │ Prevents writes to / (except mounted │  │
  │  │ : true             │ volumes at specific mountPaths)      │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ capabilities.drop  │ Removes Linux capabilities — even    │  │
  │  │ : ["ALL"]          │ processes running as root (UID 0)    │  │
  │  │                    │ lose dangerous privileges            │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ allowPrivilegeEsc  │ Prevents child processes from gaining │  │
  │  │ alation: false     │ more privileges than parent process  │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ seccompProfile     │ Filters which Linux system calls the │  │
  │  │ : RuntimeDefault   │ container can make (~300→~60 syscalls)│  │
  │  └────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The anihpj-api container runs as UID 1000 (non-root), with readOnlyRootFilesystem: true (writes only go to mounted volumes for ephemeral data), drop: ["ALL"] capabilities with only NET_BIND_SERVICE added (to bind to port 8000), and allowPrivilegeEscalation: false. Even if an attacker compromises the Django app, they cannot install tools, modify system files, or escalate to root — the blast radius is contained. Options A, B, and C control unrelated concerns: NetworkPolicy controls network traffic, ResourceQuota controls resource consumption, and PodDisruptionBudget controls availability during disruptions.

Observability & Security Q104 of 200
The anihpj platform stores database passwords, API keys, and TLS certificate private keys in Kubernetes Secrets. A security audit reveals that Secrets in etcd are stored as plaintext base64 (NOT encrypted). What must the platform team configure to encrypt Secrets before they are written to etcd?
A) Enable etcd TLS — encrypting the connection between the API server and etcd with TLS certificates also encrypts the data stored inside etcd's database files on disk
B) Configure EncryptionConfiguration — a YAML manifest passed to the API server via --encryption-provider-config flag that specifies which Kubernetes resources to encrypt at rest (e.g., secrets, configmaps) and which encryption provider to use (e.g., aescbc with a 32-byte key, aesgcm, secretbox, or kms for external key management). Without this, Secrets are stored as base64-encoded plaintext in etcd
C) Run kubectl encrypt secrets --all — Kubernetes provides a built-in CLI command that retroactively encrypts all existing Secrets in etcd using the cluster's default encryption key
D) Store Secrets in ConfigMaps with encrypted: true annotation — ConfigMaps support an encryption annotation that the API server checks before writing to etcd, making them more secure than Secrets for sensitive data
✅ Correct Answer: B

By default, Kubernetes Secrets are stored as base64-encoded plaintext in etcd. Base64 is encoding, NOT encryption — anyone with access to etcd (or its backups) can decode the values. The EncryptionConfiguration resource, passed to the API server via the --encryption-provider-config flag, enables encryption at rest. When configured, the API server encrypts Secrets with the specified provider BEFORE writing to etcd, and decrypts them AFTER reading. The encryption is transparent to users — kubectl get secret returns decoded, decrypted values. However, existing Secrets are NOT automatically encrypted — you must run kubectl get secrets --all-namespaces -o json | kubectl replace -f - to trigger re-write through the now-encrypting API server.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secrets Encryption at Rest — Before vs After EncryptionConfiguration
  WITHOUT EncryptionConfiguration (DEFAULT — DANGEROUS!):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl create secret generic db-pass --from-literal=pw=hunter2│
  │    ↓                                                            │
  │  API Server receives: pw=hunter2                                │
  │    ↓ base64-encode (NOT encryption!)                            │
  │  etcd stores: {"data":{"pw":"aHVudGVyMg=="}}  ← Decodeable!     │
  │    ↓ Anyone with etcdctl access can read:                        │
  │  etcdctl get /registry/secrets/anihpj/db-pass | base64 -d = pw! │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH EncryptionConfiguration:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Step 1: Create EncryptionConfiguration YAML                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ apiVersion: apiserver.config.k8s.io/v1                    │   │
  │  │ kind: EncryptionConfiguration                             │   │
  │  │ resources:                                                │   │
  │  │   - resources: ["secrets"]                                │   │
  │  │     providers:                                            │   │
  │  │       - aescbc:  ← Strongest provider goes FIRST          │   │
  │  │           keys:                                           │   │
  │  │             - name: key1                                  │   │
  │  │               secret: <base64-encoded-32-byte-key>        │   │
  │  │       - identity: {}  ← Fallback for reading old secrets  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Step 2: Configure API Server                                    │
  │  /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml:                  │
  │  --encryption-provider-config=/etc/kubernetes/encryption.yaml    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Step 3: API Server now encrypts BEFORE writing to etcd          │
  │  kubectl create secret → API Server → AES-CBC encrypt → etcd    │
  │  kubectl get secret    → etcd → API Server → decrypt → user     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  PROVIDERS (strongest → weakest):                                │
  │  ┌────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ kms            │ External Key Management (AWS KMS, etc.) │   │
  │  │ secretbox      │ XSalsa20 + Poly1305 (fast, no padding)  │   │
  │  │ aesgcm         │ AES-GCM (authenticated encryption)      │   │
  │  │ aescbc         │ AES-CBC with PKCS#7 padding              │   │
  │  │ identity       │ NO encryption (plaintext — only for     │   │
  │  │                │ fallback/decryption of old resources)    │   │
  │  └────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ CRITICAL: After enabling, ROTATE all existing Secrets:      │
  │  kubectl get secrets --all-namespaces -o json |                  │
  │    kubectl replace -f -   (re-writes through encrypting API)     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The platform team creates an EncryptionConfiguration using aescbc with a 32-byte key stored in a file on the control plane node (mounted into the API server static pod via hostPath). All Secrets — database passwords, API keys, TLS private keys — are now encrypted at rest in etcd. Option A is wrong because TLS encrypts data in transit (network), not at rest on disk — etcd still stores plaintext on the filesystem. Option C is wrong because there is no kubectl encrypt secrets command — rotation is done by re-writing Secrets through the now-encrypting API server. Option D is wrong because ConfigMaps should NEVER store sensitive data — they have no encryption support and are designed for non-sensitive configuration.

Observability & Security Q105 of 200
The anihpj platform team wants to add container runtime security. They've heard of seccomp, AppArmor, and SELinux, but they're confused about what each one does and how they differ. Which statement correctly describes how these three runtime security tools compare?
A) All three are interchangeable — they all do the same thing (filter syscalls) but are named differently on different Linux distributions; pick whichever your distribution supports
B) You should enable all three on every container — they stack on top of each other with no performance impact, and more security is always better regardless of the application's actual requirements
C) Seccomp filters which Linux system calls a container can make (e.g., block mount, ptrace, unshare) — defined via JSON profiles at the pod level. AppArmor restricts which files, capabilities, and network operations a process can access — defined via profiles loaded on the node, applied per-container via annotations or securityContext. SELinux applies mandatory access control (MAC) labels to every process, file, and network port — all access decisions are checked against policies at the kernel level. They operate at DIFFERENT layers and can complement each other
D) Seccomp is the only one relevant to Kubernetes — AppArmor and SELinux are legacy Linux tools that have been replaced by Kubernetes-native Pod Security Standards (PSS) and should no longer be used
✅ Correct Answer: C

These three Linux security modules operate at different layers and complement each other — they are NOT interchangeable. Seccomp (Secure Computing Mode) operates at the syscall level: it filters which Linux system calls a container can invoke. Think of it as a firewall for syscalls — "allow read/write, block mount/ptrace." Kubernetes supports seccomp via securityContext.seccompProfile (GA since v1.27). AppArmor operates at the file/resource level: it restricts which files, directories, capabilities, and network operations a program can access based on a per-program profile. Think of it as "this nginx binary can only read /var/www/html and bind port 80." SELinux operates at the kernel MAC level: every process, file, port, and resource gets a label, and the kernel enforces access rules between labels. Think of it as "processes with label X can only read files with label Y."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Seccomp vs AppArmor vs SELinux — Three Layers of Runtime Security
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            THREE LAYERS OF LINUX CONTAINER SECURITY              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │  🔒 SELINUX (Mandatory Access Control — Broadest)           ││
  │  │                                                              ││
  │  │  Layer: Kernel MAC — EVERY resource has a label             ││
  │  │  Type: Label-based access control (type enforcement)        ││
  │  │  Scope: Processes, files, sockets, ports, directories       ││
  │  │  Example: "Container with label c123 can only read files    ││
  │  │            with label f456 on ports with label p789"        ││
  │  │  K8s: seLinuxOptions in securityContext                      ││
  │  │  ⚡ Strongest isolation, highest complexity                  ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │  🛡️ APPARMOR (Path-Based Access Control — Medium)          ││
  │  │                                                              ││
  │  │  Layer: Application — restricts what a PROGRAM can access   ││
  │  │  Type: Path-based profiles (allow/deny by file path)        ││
  │  │  Scope: Files, capabilities, network, mounts, signals       ││
  │  │  Example: "/usr/bin/nginx { /var/www/html/ r,               ││
  │  │            /etc/nginx/** r, network inet tcp, }"            ││
  │  │  K8s: container.apparmor.security.beta.kubernetes.io        ││
  │  │  ⚡ Medium complexity, moderate coverage                     ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │  🔧 SECCOMP (System Call Filtering — Most Targeted)         ││
  │  │                                                              ││
  │  │  Layer: Kernel — filters which SYSCALLS are allowed         ││
  │  │  Type: BPF/JSON profiles (allow list or deny list)          ││
  │  │  Scope: Linux system calls (~300+ available)                ││
  │  │  Example: "Allow: read, write, open, close, fstat,          ││
  │  │            mmap, mprotect; Deny: mount, ptrace, unshare"    ││
  │  │  K8s: securityContext.seccompProfile (GA in 1.27)           ││
  │  │  ⚡ Low complexity, high impact (RuntimeDefault blocks       ││
  │  │      ~80% of dangerous syscalls with zero config!)          ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                 │
  │  QUICK COMPARISON:                                               │
  │  ┌──────────┬───────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────┐│
  │  │          │ Seccomp       │ AppArmor      │ SELinux          ││
  │  ├──────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤│
  │  │ What?    │ Syscall filter│ Path/profile  │ Label-based MAC  ││
  │  │ Scope    │ Syscalls only │ Files/caps/net│ Everything       ││
  │  │ Default  │ Unconfined    │ Unconfined    │ Enforcing (RHEL) ││
  │  │ K8s GA?  │ ✅ v1.27      │ ✅ v1.30      │ ✅ stable        ││
  │  │ Config   │ JSON profile  │ Profile file  │ Policy on node   ││
  │  └──────────┴───────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────┘│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The platform team starts with Seccomp RuntimeDefault on every pod (zero config, blocks dangerous syscalls like mount, ptrace). For the anihpj-api pod they add an AppArmor profile that restricts the Django process to only read /app/, write to /tmp/, and bind port 8000 — preventing shell injection from accessing /etc/passwd or spawning reverse shells. They skip SELinux on their Ubuntu nodes (AppArmor is the Ubuntu default). Option A is wrong — they operate at completely different layers and are not interchangeable. Option B is wrong — stacking all three does have performance impact and should be based on threat model, not blind application. Option D is wrong — PSS/PSA enforces pod-level settings like runAsNonRoot and readOnlyRootFilesystem, but does NOT replace Linux security modules (LSMs) like AppArmor and SELinux which operate at the kernel level.

Observability & Security Q106 of 200
At 3 AM, someone execs into the anihpj-api production pod and spawns a bash shell. The platform team needs real-time runtime threat detection — a tool that monitors system calls at the kernel level and alerts when suspicious behavior occurs (unexpected shells, sensitive file reads, outbound connections). Which tool is designed specifically for this, and how does it work?
A) Prometheus with Alertmanager — scrapes HTTP metrics endpoints every 30 seconds, evaluates PromQL alerting rules, and sends alerts to PagerDuty when thresholds are exceeded. Excellent for metric-based alerting but does NOT monitor kernel-level system calls inside containers
B) Falco — a CNCF Graduated runtime security tool that hooks into the Linux kernel (via eBPF probe or kernel module) to monitor ALL system calls in real-time. Rules are written in YAML and define conditions like "shell spawned in container," "unexpected outbound network connection," or "sensitive file read (/etc/shadow)." When a rule triggers, Falco outputs an alert to stdout, file, HTTP webhook, or gRPC — enabling integration with SIEMs, notification systems, and incident response workflows. It is the standard runtime threat detection tool for Kubernetes and appears on the CKS exam
C) kubectl logs --follow — streams container stdout/stderr logs in real-time, allowing operators to watch for suspicious log entries like "Unauthorized access" or error stack traces. Application logs are the primary runtime monitoring tool
D) kube-bench — runs CIS Benchmark compliance checks against the cluster configuration (API server flags, kubelet settings, etcd configuration) and reports on security misconfigurations. It detects configuration drift but does not monitor live runtime behavior inside running containers
✅ Correct Answer: B

Falco is the CNCF standard for Kubernetes runtime threat detection. It operates at the kernel level — not at the application log level, not at the metrics level, not at the config audit level. Falco instruments the Linux kernel (via eBPF probe or kernel module) to intercept every system call made by every process in every container. It then evaluates these syscalls against a set of rules (written in YAML) that define what constitutes suspicious behavior. When a rule matches — like "a shell was spawned inside a production container" — Falco generates an alert with rich context: which pod, which container, which process, which user, which syscall, and the full command line.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Falco Architecture — Kernel-Level Runtime Threat Detection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    FALCO — HOW IT DETECTS THREATS                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  1. KERNEL INSTRUMENTATION                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  eBPF Probe (modern, recommended)                        │   │
  │  │  OR Kernel Module (legacy)                               │   │
  │  │    ↓ Every syscall from every container flows through    │   │
  │  │    ↓ Falco's kernel instrumentation point                │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  2. FALCO ENGINE — Event Stream Processing                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Syscall Events: execve, open, connect, mkdir, chmod...  │   │
  │  │    ↓ Enriched with K8s metadata                           │   │
  │  │    ↓ (pod name, namespace, container ID, labels)         │   │
  │  │    ↓ Rule Evaluation Engine                               │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3. RULE EXAMPLE — "Shell Spawned in Container"                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ - rule: Shell in Container                               │   │
  │  │   desc: Detect bash/sh spawned in a running container    │   │
  │  │   condition: >                                           │   │
  │  │     spawned_process                                      │   │
  │  │     and container                                        │   │
  │  │     and proc.name in (bash, sh, zsh)                     │   │
  │  │     and not proc.args contains "healthy-check.sh"        │   │
  │  │   output: >                                              │   │
  │  │     Shell spawned in container (user=%user.name          │   │
  │  │     pod=%k8s.pod.name ns=%k8s.ns.name                    │   │
  │  │     parent=%proc.pname cmdline=%proc.cmdline)            │   │
  │  │   priority: WARNING                                       │   │
  │  │   tags: [container, shell, mitre_execution]              │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  4. OUTPUT CHANNELS                                             │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  stdout    → kubectl logs falco-pod                       │   │
  │  │  file      → /var/log/falco/events.json                  │   │
  │  │  HTTP POST → SIEM (Splunk, Elastic), webhook (Slack)     │   │
  │  │  gRPC      → Falcosidekick → 50+ integrations            │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CKS EXAM — CRITICAL FALCO RULES TO KNOW:                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • Terminal shell in container                               │ │
  │  │ • Read sensitive file (/etc/shadow, /etc/passwd)            │ │
  │  │ • Write below /etc or /root (container tampering)           │ │
  │  │ • Contact K8s API server from container                     │ │
  │  │ • Launch privileged container or mount sensitive host path  │ │
  │  │ • Unexpected outbound network connection (netcat, reverse)  │ │
  │  │ • Change thread namespace (container escape attempt)        │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Falco is deployed as a DaemonSet on every node. Custom rules detect: (1) shell spawned in anihpj-api or anihpj-db containers, (2) unexpected outbound connections from anihpj-web to external IPs, and (3) reads of /etc/kubernetes/pki/ from non-control-plane components. Alerts are sent to Slack (#anihpj-security) via Falcosidekick and also logged to a file collected by Loki for dashboarding in Grafana. Option A (Prometheus) monitors metrics, not syscalls — it can't see a shell being spawned. Option C (kubectl logs) only shows what the application logs deliberately — an attacker spawning a shell doesn't log that to stdout.
