Option D (kube-bench) audits static configuration, not runtime behavior — it runs once and reports, it doesn't monitor continuously.

2.5 Security in CI/CD PipelinesQ107-Q112

Observability & Security Q107 of 200
The anihpj team currently runs security scans only before production deployment — a manual step where a security engineer reviews the Docker image and YAML manifests. Every release, they discover critical vulnerabilities that delay the release by 2-3 days. What "Shift-Left Security" approach should the platform team implement to catch these issues earlier?
A) Integrate automated security checks at every stage of the CI pipeline — SAST (static analysis) on every git push, dependency scanning on every PR, container image vulnerability scanning (Trivy) at build time, manifest validation (kube-linter, conftest) before deploy, and image signature verification (Cosign/Kyverno) at admission time. This moves security from a "gate at the end" to a "continuous feedback loop" that catches issues within minutes of code being written — when they're cheapest to fix
B) Hire two additional security engineers to review every PR manually before merge — increasing the team size is the most reliable way to catch vulnerabilities, since automated tools produce too many false positives
C) Run security scans only on the quarterly release branch — since major releases happen quarterly, scanning more frequently is wasteful and slows down development velocity unnecessarily
D) Accept the 2-3 day delay as normal — security reviews inherently take time, and any attempt to automate them will miss critical vulnerabilities that only human experts can find
✅ Correct Answer: A

Shift-Left Security means moving security checks earlier in the development lifecycle — from "right before production" to "as soon as code is written." The principle: the earlier you catch a vulnerability, the cheaper and faster it is to fix. A vulnerability found during coding costs minutes; the same vulnerability found in production costs days and may have already been exploited. The modern approach layers multiple automated tools at each pipeline stage: commit → lint + SAST → PR → dependency scan + secret detection → build → image vulnerability scan → deploy → manifest validation + image signature verification → runtime → Falco threat detection.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Shift-Left Security — Multiple Gates Across the CI/CD Pipeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SHIFT-LEFT SECURITY — Catch Issues EARLY (when cheap to fix)    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TRADITIONAL (BROKEN):                                          │
  │  Code → Build → Test → Deploy → ⛔ SECURITY REVIEW (2-3 days)   │
  │  ↑ Vulnerabilities found HERE — expensive rework, delayed release│
  │                                                                 │
  │  SHIFT-LEFT (CORRECT):                                           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌────┐│
  │  │ COMMIT  │──▶│   PR    │──▶│  BUILD  │──▶│ DEPLOY  │──▶│RUN ││
  │  │         │   │         │   │         │   │         │   │    ││
  │  │ 🔒 SAST │   │ 🔒 Dep  │   │ 🔒 Image│   │ 🔒 K8s  │   │ 🔒 ││
  │  │ bandit  │   │  Scan   │   │  Scan   │   │  Valid  │   │Falco││
  │  │ ruff    │   │ pip-aud │   │  Trivy  │   │ kube-lint│   │RT   ││
  │  │ mypy    │   │ safety  │   │  Syft   │   │ conftest │   │Det  ││
  │  └────┬────┘   └────┬────┘   └────┬────┘   └────┬────┘   └────┘│
  │       │             │             │             │               │
  │       ▼             ▼             ▼             ▼               │
  │  ❌ FAIL if:    ❌ FAIL if:    ❌ FAIL if:    ❌ FAIL if:        │
  │  • SQLi vuln   • CVE in dep  • CRITICAL CVE • privileged pod   │
  │  • hardcoded   • outdated    • no SBOM     • no resource       │
  │    secret      • known vuln  • unsigned    • limits missing    │
  │                             image                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  COST OF FIXING A VULNERABILITY:                                │
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Stage Found  │ Fix Time │ Relative Cost                    │ │
  │  ├──────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ IDE/coding   │ minutes  │ $ (baseline)                     │ │
  │  │ CI pipeline  │ hours    │ $$ (10x)                         │ │
  │  │ Staging      │ days     │ $$$ (50x)                        │ │
  │  │ Production   │ days-weeks│ $$$$ (100-1000x, + breach risk) │ │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Every git push triggers bandit (Python SAST) and ruff (linting). Every PR triggers pip-audit (dependency scanning) and detects hardcoded secrets with detect-secrets. Every image build triggers Trivy (vulnerability scan) and Syft (SBOM generation). Every deployment triggers Kyverno (policy validation — no privileged pods, must have limits) and Cosign verification (only signed images allowed). If ANY gate fails, the pipeline stops and the developer gets feedback within minutes — not days. Option B is wrong because manual review doesn't scale and is slower than automated tools. Option C is wrong because quarterly scanning leaves you vulnerable for months between scans. Option D is wrong because automated tools catch different classes of issues than humans — they complement each other, not replace each other.

Observability & Security Q108 of 200
The anihpj CI pipeline builds a Docker image from python:3.12-slim. Before pushing to the container registry, the platform team wants to scan the image for known CVEs (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures) in installed OS packages and Python dependencies. Which tool should they integrate, and how should the pipeline respond to findings?
A) docker scout — a Docker-specific tool that only scans images stored in Docker Hub registries; cannot scan images in AWS ECR, GCR, or private Harbor registries used by anihpj
B) SonarQube — a static code analysis tool that scans source code for code quality issues and bugs; it analyzes code patterns (SQL injection, null pointers) but does not scan container images for OS-level CVEs
C) Falco — a runtime threat detection tool that monitors system calls in running containers; it detects suspicious BEHAVIOR at runtime but does not scan container images at BUILD time for known vulnerabilities
D) Trivy — an open-source vulnerability scanner by Aqua Security that scans container images, filesystems, and Git repositories for known CVEs. It compares installed packages (apt, pip, npm, gem, etc.) against multiple vulnerability databases (NVD, GitHub Advisory, Red Hat, Debian, Alpine). CI integration: trivy image --severity CRITICAL,HIGH --exit-code 1 anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 — the --exit-code 1 flag FAILS the pipeline if critical/high CVEs are found, preventing vulnerable images from reaching production
✅ Correct Answer: D

Trivy is the most widely used open-source container image vulnerability scanner in the cloud native ecosystem. It works by: (1) extracting the list of installed packages from the container image layers (apt for Debian/Ubuntu, apk for Alpine, pip for Python, npm for Node.js, etc.), (2) comparing each package version against multiple vulnerability databases (NVD, GitHub Advisory Database, distro-specific trackers), and (3) reporting any matches with severity levels (CRITICAL, HIGH, MEDIUM, LOW). The key CI integration pattern is --exit-code 1 — this makes the scan FAIL the pipeline when vulnerabilities at specified severity levels are found, creating a hard gate that prevents vulnerable images from being pushed to the registry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trivy Image Scanning — CI Pipeline Integration Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          TRIVY — CONTAINER IMAGE VULNERABILITY SCANNING          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CI PIPELINE INTEGRATION:                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  # Step 1: Build the image                                │   │
  │  │  docker build -t anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 .                  │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Step 2: SCAN the image BEFORE pushing to registry      │   │
  │  │  trivy image \                                            │   │
  │  │    --severity CRITICAL,HIGH \   ← Only fail on these      │   │
  │  │    --exit-code 1 \               ← FAIL pipeline if found │   │
  │  │    --format table \              ← Human-readable output  │   │
  │  │    --ignore-unfixed \            ← Only fixed CVEs matter │   │
  │  │    anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3                                 │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Step 3: Only push if scan passes (exit code = 0)       │   │
  │  │  docker push anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3                        │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WHAT TRIVY SCANS:                                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Package Type    Example              Vulnerability DB     │ │
  │  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  OS (apt)       libssl1.1 → CVE-2023-XXXX   Debian/Ubuntu │ │
  │  │  OS (apk)       openssl → CVE-2024-XXXX     Alpine         │ │
  │  │  Python (pip)   django==4.2.1 → CVE-...     GitHub Advisory│ │
  │  │  Node.js (npm)  express → GHSA-...          NVD            │ │
  │  │  Go (modules)   net/http → CVE-...          Red Hat        │ │
  │  │  Java (jar)     log4j → CVE-2021-44228      Multiple       │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TRIVY OUTPUT (example — anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3):                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 (debian 12.5)                     │   │
  │  │  ========================================================│   │
  │  │  Total: 3 (CRITICAL: 1, HIGH: 2)                         │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┬──────────────┬──────────┬───────────────┐  │   │
  │  │  │ LIBRARY  │ VULNERABILITY│ SEVERITY │ FIXED VERSION │  │   │
  │  │  ├──────────┼──────────────┼──────────┼───────────────┤  │   │
  │  │  │ django   │ CVE-2024-... │ CRITICAL │ 4.2.13        │  │   │
  │  │  │ libexpat │ CVE-2023-... │ HIGH     │ 2.5.0-1+deb12 │  │   │
  │  │  │ openssl  │ CVE-2024-... │ HIGH     │ 3.0.13-1      │  │   │
  │  │  └──────────┴──────────────┴──────────┴───────────────┘  │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  ❌ exit code: 1 — PIPELINE BLOCKED!                      │   │
  │  │  Action: Upgrade django to 4.2.13 and rebuild             │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ALTERNATIVES: Trivy is the CNCF standard, but similar tools:    │
  │  • Grype (Anchore) — similar functionality, different DB         │
  │  • Snyk — commercial, richer UI, integrates with Snyk platform   │
  │  • Docker Scout — Docker-specific, tight Docker Hub integration  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The CI pipeline runs Trivy after every image build. Policy: --severity CRITICAL,HIGH --exit-code 1 blocks the push on critical/high CVEs. Medium/Low CVEs are logged as warnings but don't block. The Trivy results are also stored as a SARIF file for GitHub's Security tab, giving developers visibility into their dependencies' security posture. Option A is wrong because Docker Scout works with any OCI-compliant registry, not just Docker Hub — but Trivy is the CNCF standard and more widely adopted. Option B is wrong because SonarQube analyzes source code quality, not container image packages — it can't see OS-level CVEs. Option C is wrong because Falco monitors runtime behavior, not build-time image contents — it can see a shell being spawned but can't see an unpatched libssl in the image.

Observability & Security Q109 of 200
The anihpj platform team builds container images in CI and pushes them to ECR. They want to ensure that ONLY images built by their trusted CI pipeline can run in production — even if an attacker gains access to the ECR repository and pushes a malicious image. What supply chain security mechanism provides this guarantee?
A) Image tag immutability — configure ECR to reject overwrites of existing image tags (e.g., prevent pushing a new image to v1.2.3 once it's been tagged). This prevents tag mutation but does NOT verify WHO built the image
B) Cosign (part of Sigstore) for image signing and Kyverno verifyImages for admission-time enforcement. The CI pipeline signs the image with a private key after a successful build + scan: cosign sign --key cosign.key anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3. This creates a cryptographic signature stored in the OCI registry alongside the image. Kyverno is configured with a ClusterPolicy that runs verifyImages at admission time — if the image is unsigned or the signature doesn't match the platform's public key, the pod creation is REJECTED. This guarantees ONLY CI-built images run in the cluster
C) Docker Content Trust (DCT) — uses Notary v1 to sign images with Docker's legacy signing system. DCT is deprecated and replaced by Notary v2 / Sigstore Cosign, and is not supported by modern OCI registries
D) AWS IAM resource policies on the ECR repository — restrict which IAM roles can push images to ECR. This prevents unauthorized pushes but does NOT verify the integrity of images that were legitimately pushed (e.g., if the CI pipeline itself is compromised)
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cosign + Kyverno verifyImages creates a cryptographic chain of trust from CI pipeline to running pod. The workflow: (1) CI builds + scans the image, (2) CI signs the image digest with a private key using Cosign — this creates a signature attestation stored in the OCI registry, (3) Kyverno ClusterPolicy intercepts every pod creation request via ValidatingAdmissionWebhook, (4) Kyverno pulls the image's signatures from the registry and verifies them against the platform's public key, (5) only signed images matching the trusted key are allowed to run. This prevents BOTH external attackers (pushing unsigned images) and compromised CI pipelines (signing with wrong key).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cosign Image Signing + Kyverno Admission Enforcement
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       IMAGE SIGNING & VERIFICATION — END-TO-END TRUST CHAIN      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STEP 1: SIGN THE IMAGE IN CI (Build-Time)                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  CI Pipeline (GitHub Actions):                            │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Build + scan + push the image                          │   │
  │  │  docker build -t anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 .                  │   │
  │  │  docker push anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3                        │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Sign the image DIGEST (not tag — digests are immutable)│   │
  │  │  cosign sign \                                           │   │
  │  │    --key cosign.key \          ← Private key (CI secret)  │   │
  │  │    anihpj/jobpost@sha256:abc123...  ← Digest, not tag    │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  ✅ Signature stored in ECR alongside the image           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STEP 2: ENFORCE AT ADMISSION (Deploy-Time)                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Kyverno ClusterPolicy (runs on every pod create/update): │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                 │   │
  │  │  kind: ClusterPolicy                                       │   │
  │  │  metadata:                                                 │   │
  │  │    name: verify-anihpj-images                              │   │
  │  │  spec:                                                     │   │
  │  │    validationFailureAction: Enforce  ← BLOCK unsigned     │   │
  │  │    rules:                                                  │   │
  │  │    - name: check-image-signature                           │   │
  │  │      match:                                                │   │
  │  │        resources:                                          │   │
  │  │          kinds: [Pod]                                      │   │
  │  │      verifyImages:                                         │   │
  │  │      - imageReferences:                                    │   │
  │  │        - "anihpj/jobpost:*"                                │   │
  │  │        attestors:                                          │   │
  │  │        - entries:                                          │   │
  │  │          - key: |-                  ← Public key           │   │
  │  │              -----BEGIN PUBLIC KEY-----                    │   │
  │  │              MFkwEwYHKoZ...                               │   │
  │  │              -----END PUBLIC KEY-----                      │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STEP 3: WHAT HAPPENS AT DEPLOY TIME                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  kubectl apply -f anihpj-deploy.yaml                       │   │
  │  │    ↓ API Server receives request                           │   │
  │  │    ↓ Kyverno webhook intercepts                            │   │
  │  │    ↓ Kyverno pulls signature from ECR                      │   │
  │  │    ↓ Kyverno verifies signature against public key         │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  ✅ SIGNATURE VALID → Pod created                          │   │
  │  │  ❌ NO SIGNATURE → Pod REJECTED (attacker blocked!)       │   │
  │  │  ❌ WRONG KEY   → Pod REJECTED (compromised CI blocked!)  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  KEYLESS SIGNING (Sigstore — no key management!):                │
  │  cosign sign --fulcio-url=https://fulcio.sigstore.dev \         │
  │    --rekor-url=https://rekor.sigstore.dev \                     │
  │    anihpj/jobpost@sha256:abc123...                               │
  │  → Uses OIDC identity (GitHub Actions token) instead of keys    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The CI pipeline uses Cosign with a key pair stored as GitHub Actions secrets. After every successful build + scan, the image digest is signed. Kyverno's verifyImages rule runs at admission time — if anyone tries to deploy an unsigned anihpj image (whether from a compromised ECR push, a typo-squatted image, or a developer's local build), Kyverno rejects it. This closes the gap between "the image was pushed to ECR" and "the image was built by OUR pipeline." Option A prevents tag mutation but doesn't verify provenance — an attacker could push v1.2.4 with a malicious image. Option C is wrong because DCT/Notary v1 is legacy and replaced by Cosign/Sigstore. Option D controls WHO can push but doesn't verify WHAT was pushed — if CI credentials are compromised, IAM policies don't help.

Observability & Security Q110 of 200
A zero-day vulnerability (Log4Shell-level severity) is announced. The anihpj security team needs to know IMMEDIATELY whether any of their 47 running container images contain the vulnerable library — across all environments (dev, staging, prod). What supply chain artifact would enable them to answer this question in seconds without re-scanning every image?
A) Dockerfile — the Dockerfile lists the base image and installed packages, so reading it tells you exactly what libraries are in the final image. No separate artifact is needed
B) Container image labels — adding a LABEL to the Dockerfile with the list of installed packages provides a quick lookup without additional tooling. Standard Docker inspect can read labels
C) SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) — a machine-readable inventory of every component, library, and dependency in a software artifact. For container images, tools like Syft generate SBOMs in standard formats (SPDX, CycloneDX) at build time. The SBOM is stored alongside the image in the OCI registry (as an attestation) or in a central SBOM database. When a new CVE is announced, the security team queries the SBOM database: "Which images contain log4j-core version 2.0–2.14.1?" — answer in seconds. SBOMs are a cornerstone of supply chain security and are mandated by US Executive Order 14028 for federal software
D) kubectl describe pod — running this command shows the exact image digest and tag for every running pod, which is sufficient to determine which images need to be patched when a vulnerability is announced
✅ Correct Answer: C

An SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is like a nutrition label for software — it lists every ingredient inside. For a container image, the SBOM includes: the base OS packages (apt/apk), language-level dependencies (pip, npm, maven, go modules), and any files copied into the image. SBOMs are generated at build time by tools like Syft (Anchore), which introspects the image filesystem and outputs a structured document in standard formats: SPDX (Linux Foundation standard, ISO/IEC 5962) or CycloneDX (OWASP standard). Without SBOMs, when a new CVE is announced, every team must re-scan every image — taking hours or days. With SBOMs, a single database query answers the question in seconds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SBOM — Software Bill of Materials Generation & Usage
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          SBOM — KNOW EXACTLY WHAT'S INSIDE EVERY IMAGE           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STEP 1: GENERATE SBOM AT BUILD TIME (CI Pipeline)               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  # After docker build, before docker push                 │   │
  │  │  syft anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 \                            │   │
  │  │    -o spdx-json \               ← SPDX JSON format        │   │
  │  │    > sbom-spdx.json                                     │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Or CycloneDX format (OWASP standard)                   │   │
  │  │  syft anihpj/jobpost:v1.2.3 \                            │   │
  │  │    -o cyclonedx-json \                                   │   │
  │  │    > sbom-cyclonedx.json                                 │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STEP 2: STORE SBOM (Options)                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Option A: Attach to image in OCI registry                │   │
  │  │    cosign attach sbom --sbom sbom-spdx.json \            │   │
  │  │      anihpj/jobpost@sha256:abc123...                      │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  Option B: Central SBOM database                          │   │
  │  │    Dependency-Track, Anchore Enterprise, or custom DB     │   │
  │  │    → Upload SBOM → database indexes every component       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STEP 3: QUERY WHEN CVE IS ANNOUNCED                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🚨 CVE-2024-XXXX announced: log4j-core 2.0-2.14.1 RCE   │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  Query SBOM database:                                     │   │
  │  │  "SELECT image, version                                    │   │
  │  │   FROM sboms                                               │   │
  │  │   WHERE component = 'log4j-core'                           │   │
  │  │   AND version >= '2.0' AND version <= '2.14.1'"          │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  Result (in SECONDS):                                      │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────┬──────────┐        │   │
  │  │  │ IMAGE                │ ENVIRONMENT │ VERSION  │        │   │
  │  │  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────┤        │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-api:v2.1.0    │ prod        │ 2.14.0   │ ❌ VULN │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-api:v2.1.1    │ staging     │ 2.15.0   │ ✅ SAFE │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-worker:v1.5.0 │ prod        │ 2.14.1   │ ❌ VULN │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-web:v3.0.2    │ prod        │ N/A      │ ✅ SAFE │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────────┴─────────────┴──────────┘        │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  ACTION: Immediately patch anihpj-api:v2.1.0 + worker     │   │
  │  │          No need to re-scan 47 images — SBOMs had answers │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SBOM STANDARDS:                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ SPDX        │ Linux Foundation     │ ISO/IEC 5962:2021      │ │
  │  │ CycloneDX   │ OWASP                │ SBOM + Vulnerability   │ │
  │  │ Syft        │ Anchore (tool)       │ Generates SPDX/CycloneDX│ │
  │  └─────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  US Executive Order 14028 (2021):                                │
  │  "Software suppliers must provide SBOMs for federal software"    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Every CI build generates an SBOM with Syft in SPDX format. The SBOM is attached to the image in ECR via Cosign attach sbom. All SBOMs are also uploaded to Dependency-Track, a central database that continuously correlates SBOM components against known CVEs. When a new CVE drops, the security team queries Dependency-Track and has answers in seconds — no re-scanning needed. Option A is wrong because a Dockerfile only shows the INTENT (what you asked to install), not the ACTUAL result — transitive dependencies, OS package updates, and build-time changes are invisible. Option B is wrong because labels are manual, get stale, and can't represent the full dependency tree with versions. Option D is wrong because knowing the image digest tells you WHICH image, but not WHAT'S INSIDE it — you'd still need to scan it.

Observability & Security Q111 of 200
The anihpj CI pipeline needs to push container images to AWS ECR. The current setup uses long-lived AWS IAM access keys stored as GitHub Actions secrets. The platform team wants to eliminate static credentials from the pipeline entirely. What modern approach achieves this?
A) Rotate the AWS access keys every 30 days using a cron job in the pipeline — shorter-lived keys with automatic rotation reduce the risk of key leakage
B) OIDC (OpenID Connect) federation — GitHub Actions acts as an OIDC identity provider. The pipeline's GitHub token is exchanged for a short-lived AWS IAM role credential via configure-aws-credentials action. AWS trusts GitHub's OIDC provider to vouch for the pipeline's identity (repo + branch + environment). No static AWS keys are ever stored — credentials are ephemeral, lasting only for the duration of the workflow run. Key setup: AWS IAM OIDC identity provider trusting token.actions.githubusercontent.com + IAM role with trust policy scoped to specific repos
C) Store AWS credentials in a Kubernetes Secret and mount it into the CI runner pod — Kubernetes Secrets are encrypted at rest in etcd after configuring EncryptionConfiguration, so they are secure enough for pipeline credentials
D) Use AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store as a credential broker — the pipeline calls SSM at runtime to retrieve the AWS access keys, which are rotated automatically by AWS. This way, the pipeline never stores credentials itself
✅ Correct Answer: B

OIDC federation eliminates static credentials from CI/CD pipelines by replacing long-lived access keys with short-lived, automatically-generated tokens that are valid only for a single workflow run. The flow: (1) GitHub generates an OIDC token for the workflow with claims like aud: sts.amazonaws.com, sub: repo:anihpj/jobpost:ref:refs/heads/main, (2) The configure-aws-credentials action exchanges this token with AWS STS for IAM role credentials, (3) AWS validates the token using its OIDC identity provider, checking the trust policy conditions (repo, branch, environment), (4) If valid, AWS returns temporary credentials valid for 1 hour. No secrets were stored — the "credential" was the pipeline's own identity.

📝 Detailed Explanation

For anihpj: The GitHub Actions pipeline uses configure-aws-credentials with OIDC to assume an IAM role scoped to the anihpj/jobpost repo. No AWS keys are stored as GitHub Secrets. The IAM trust policy restricts the role to workflows from the main branch only — a PR from a fork cannot assume the production role. Option A only shortens exposure but doesn't eliminate static keys — leaked keys still work until rotation. Option C moves the problem — K8s Secrets are base64, not encrypted by default. Option D is circular — SSM Parameter Store also needs AWS credentials to access, so you'd still need keys to get keys.

Observability & Security Q112 of 200
A supply chain attack compromises an anihpj upstream dependency. The platform team needs a framework to assess how tamper-proof their build pipeline is — from source code to running container. Which framework provides maturity levels (1-4) for software supply chain integrity, and what defines each level?
A) SLSA (Supply chain Levels for Software Artifacts) — a Google-originated framework now under the OpenSSF that defines 4 levels: L1 (build exists, no attestation — basic documentation), L2 (version control + build service with provenance — know WHO built it), L3 (auditable build platform with non-falsifiable attestations — PROVE who built it, source and build are tamper-resistant, security controls prevent unauthorized modifications), L4 (hermetic, reproducible, two-person review — the highest assurance level where builds are fully isolated, reproducible bit-for-bit, and require multiple approvals)
B) CIS Benchmarks — provides configuration hardening guidelines for Kubernetes components but does not assess build pipeline integrity or supply chain maturity levels
C) NIST Cybersecurity Framework (CSF) — the US government's cybersecurity maturity model with 5 functions (Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, Recover) applicable to organizations, not specifically to container build pipeline integrity
D) DORA (DevOps Research and Assessment) — measures software delivery performance (deploy frequency, lead time, MTTR, change failure rate) but does not define supply chain integrity levels
✅ Correct Answer: A

SLSA (pronounced "salsa") is THE framework for supply chain integrity. It answers: "Can I trust that this artifact was actually built from this source code, by a trusted builder, without tampering?" Each level builds on the previous: L1 is basic — the source is version-controlled and a build exists (but anyone could have built it). L2 adds signed provenance from a hosted build service (we know GitHub Actions built it, but the build steps could have been compromised). L3 adds auditable, non-falsifiable attestations and hardened build platforms (the build platform itself enforces security controls — you can PROVE the build is legitimate). L4 requires hermetic builds (no network access), reproducible builds (bit-for-bit identical), and two-person review — the gold standard for critical infrastructure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLSA Framework — Supply Chain Integrity Maturity Levels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      SLSA (SUPPLY-CHAIN LEVELS FOR SOFTWARE ARTIFACTS)           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │

  │  │  ❌ No provenance (can't prove who built it)               │ │

  │  │  ✅ Two-person review required for all changes              │ │
  │  │  🔴 Requires significant investment (critical infra only)   │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ATTACKS SLSA PREVENTS:                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Attack             │ SLSA Mitigation                      │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Compromised source │ L3: Two-person review, non-falsifiable│  │
  │  │ (push malicious    │ attestation proves source repo+commit │  │
  │  │  code to repo)     │ that was used in the build            │  │
  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Compromised CI     │ L3: In-toto attestations signed      │  │

  │  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤  │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: The platform targets SLSA Level 3. Every build runs on GitHub Actions (hosted platform) with signed Cosign attestations. The provenance document records: the exact git commit, the builder identity, all build commands, and all dependencies. Kyverno verifies these attestations at deployment time. Options B, C, and D are all real frameworks but answer different questions: CIS benchmarks (config hardening), NIST CSF (organizational cybersecurity), DORA (delivery performance).

Q113-Q144 32 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Continuous Integration Pipelines OverviewQ113-Q119

Continuous Delivery Q113 of 200
The anihpj team wants to understand the COMPLETE flow from a developer's git push to a running pod in production. Which sequence correctly describes a modern CI pipeline — from code commit to deployable artifact — with security integrated at every stage?
A) git push → docker build → docker push → kubectl apply — the minimal pipeline with no testing, scanning, or signing; fastest but no safety guarantees
B) git push → run tests → manual QA approval → security review → deploy — a traditional gated pipeline with human checkpoints at each stage; safe but slow (days per release)
C) git push → lint → test → build → scan → sign → push → update Git — a fully automated modern CI pipeline. Lint (ruff, black) catches style errors. Test (pytest) verifies correctness. Build (docker build) creates the image. Scan (Trivy) catches CVEs. Sign (Cosign) cryptographically attests provenance. Push (to ECR) stores the artifact. Update Git (ArgoCD image updater) updates the deployment manifest. Every stage is automated and provides fast feedback within minutes
D) git push → wait for nightly batch → run all tests at midnight → build next morning → deploy during maintenance window — batch-oriented pipeline for predictable, scheduled releases
✅ Correct Answer: C

The modern CI pipeline is a fully automated sequence of gates — each stage either passes (continues to next) or fails (stops and notifies the developer). There are no manual approvals or human checkpoints between stages. The pipeline should complete in under 10 minutes to provide fast feedback. Each stage catches a different class of problems: lint catches style/type errors (seconds), tests catch logic bugs (minutes), scan catches known CVEs (seconds to minutes), sign creates cryptographic trust evidence (seconds). The artifact (container image) is only pushed to the registry if ALL stages pass — this is the "if it's in the registry, it's been fully validated" guarantee.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Modern CI Pipeline — From git push to Deployable Artifact
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         ANIHPJ CI PIPELINE — GITHUB ACTIONS WORKFLOW             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  git push to main                                                │
  │       │                                                         │
  │       ▼                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  STAGE 1: LINT (30 seconds)                               │   │
  │  │  ruff check .              ← Python linter (style + bugs) │   │
  │  │  black --check .           ← Code formatter               │   │
  │  │  mypy anihpj/              ← Type checker                 │   │
  │  │  bandit -r anihpj/         ← SAST (security lint)         │   │
  │  │  ❌ FAIL → notify dev in Slack                          │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │       │ ✅ PASS                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  STAGE 2: TEST (3 minutes)                                │   │
  │  │  pytest --cov=anihpj --cov-report=xml                    │   │
  │  │  ❌ FAIL → "3 tests failed, coverage 72% (<80% gate)"   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │       │ ✅ PASS (coverage > 80%)                                 │
  │       ▼                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  STAGE 3: BUILD + SCAN + SIGN (4 minutes)                 │   │
  │  │  docker build -t anihpj/jobpost:${GITHUB_SHA} .          │   │
  │  │  trivy image --severity CRITICAL,HIGH --exit-code 1 \   │   │
  │  │    anihpj/jobpost:${GITHUB_SHA}                           │   │
  │  │  cosign sign --key cosign.key \                          │   │
  │  │    anihpj/jobpost@sha256:...                              │   │
  │  │  syft anihpj/jobpost:${GITHUB_SHA} -o spdx-json         │   │
  │  │  ❌ FAIL → "CRITICAL CVE found: django CVE-2024-..."    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │       │ ✅ ALL PASSED                                             │
  │       ▼                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  STAGE 4: PUSH + UPDATE GIT (30 seconds)                  │   │
  │  │  docker push anihpj/jobpost:${GITHUB_SHA}                │   │
  │  │  Update deployment.yaml with new image tag in GitOps repo │   │
  │  │  ✅ ArgoCD detects change → auto-syncs to staging        │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TOTAL TIME: ~8 minutes (fast feedback)                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: This exact pipeline runs on every push to main. Developers get feedback within 8 minutes. If any stage fails, the pipeline stops immediately (fail-fast) and the developer is notified. The image is ONLY pushed to ECR after passing all gates — ensuring the registry never contains untested, unscanned images. Option A skips all validation (dangerous). Option B adds manual gates (slow, doesn't scale). Option D is batch processing (feedback measured in days, not minutes).

Continuous Delivery Q114 of 200
The anihpj platform needs different CI behaviors for different scenarios: full pipeline on main push, lint+test only on PRs, nightly security scan, and manual deploy trigger for production. What CI concept enables these different trigger conditions?
A) Pipeline branching — creating separate pipeline YAML files for each scenario (ci-main.yml, ci-pr.yml, ci-nightly.yml) and switching between them manually based on the trigger
B) Pipeline triggers — event-driven rules that determine WHEN and WHICH pipeline runs. Common triggers: push (runs on every git push to specific branches — full CI on main), pull_request (runs when a PR is opened/updated — lighter CI with lint+test only, no deploy), schedule (cron-based — nightly Trivy deep scan and dependency audit), workflow_dispatch (manual — human-triggered production deployment with approval), and webhook (external system triggers — e.g., ArgoCD notifies CI of sync failure)
C) Pipeline templates — reusable YAML snippets that define shared pipeline logic and are included in each branch's configuration via an include directive
D) Pipeline environment variables — using IF conditions based on environment variables set differently per branch to selectively skip or run pipeline stages
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pipeline triggers define the event that starts a pipeline run. In GitHub Actions, the on: key specifies triggers. Different triggers can run different workflows or the same workflow with conditional steps. The key principle: match the depth of validation to the context. A PR only needs lint + test (fast feedback); a merge to main needs full CI (build + scan + sign + push); a production deploy needs manual approval; a scheduled scan catches newly-disclosed CVEs in already-built images that were clean at build time.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CI Pipeline Triggers — When and What Runs
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     PIPELINE TRIGGERS                            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TRIGGER         WHEN                    WHAT RUNS              │
  │  ───────────     ────────────────────    ────────────────────── │
  │  push:           git push to main        ✅ FULL CI: lint→test   │
  │    branches:                              →build→scan→sign→push │
  │      - main                                                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  pull_request:   PR opened/updated       ✅ LIGHT CI: lint→test  │
  │    branches:                              (faster, no deploy)   │

  │                                                                 │

  │                                                                 │
  │  workflow_       Manual button click      ✅ PROD DEPLOY: human  │
  │  dispatch:        in GitHub UI             approval required    │

For anihpj: push to main triggers full CI including build+scan+sign+push to ECR. pull_request triggers lint+test only (2 min feedback). schedule runs nightly Trivy scan on ALL images in ECR — catching CVEs discovered after build. workflow_dispatch triggers production deployment with required approver from the platform team. Options A, C, and D describe implementation details but the CONCEPT is pipeline triggers.

Continuous Delivery Q115 of 200
The anihpj CI pipeline has independent stages: lint (30s), unit tests (3min), integration tests (4min), and Docker build (3min). Running them sequentially takes 10+ minutes. How should the platform team structure these stages to reduce total pipeline time without breaking dependencies?
A) Skip unit tests and integration tests in CI — run them only in staging since they're slow; this reduces pipeline time to ~3 minutes for lint+build
B) Combine all stages into one mega-stage — a single script that runs lint, tests, and build sequentially within the same job; this eliminates overhead from job startup but doesn't actually parallelize
C) Remove lint entirely since tests will catch the same errors — linting is redundant when you have comprehensive test coverage above 80%
D) Parallelize independent stages — lint and unit tests can run simultaneously (they don't depend on each other). Integration tests can run in parallel with Docker build (they need compiled code but can use a cached build). The key is identifying the dependency graph: lint ∥ unit-tests → (both pass) → integration-tests ∥ docker-build → (both pass) → push. This reduces 10.5 minutes sequential to ~6 minutes parallel — nearly 2x faster without removing any quality gates
✅ Correct Answer: D

Pipeline parallelization runs independent stages simultaneously rather than sequentially. The key is understanding the dependency graph: Stage B depends on Stage A if B needs A's output. Independent stages (no shared output dependency) can run IN PARALLEL. Dependent stages must run SEQUENTIALLY. In CI/CD platforms like GitHub Actions, parallel jobs are defined in the same needs: level — jobs with the same dependencies run concurrently. The total pipeline time becomes the critical path (longest chain of dependent stages), not the sum of all stages.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sequential vs Parallel — Pipeline Dependency Graph
  SEQUENTIAL (10.5 min):                    PARALLEL (~6 min):
  ┌──────┐                                  ┌──────┐  ┌──────┐
  │ lint │ 30s                              │ lint │  │ unit │
  └──┬───┘                                  │ 30s  │  │ test │
     │                                      └──┬───┘  │ 3min │
  ┌──▼───┐                                     │      └──┬───┘
  │ unit │ 3min                                │         │
  │ test │                                     ├─────────┘
  └──┬───┘                                     │ both pass
     │                                         ▼
  ┌──▼──────────┐                         ┌────────────┐ ┌───────┐
  │ integration │ 4min                    │ integration │ │docker │
  │    test     │                         │    test     │ │build  │
  └──┬──────────┘                         │   4min      │ │3min   │
     │                                     └─────┬──────┘ └───┬───┘
  ┌──▼──────┐                                     │            │
  │ docker  │ 3min                                └─────┬──────┘
  │ build   │                                          │
  └──┬──────┘                                     ┌────▼────┐
     │                                             │  push   │
  ┌──▼───┐                                         │  30s    │
  │ push │ 30s                                     └─────────┘
  └──────┘
                                                  Critical path:
  Total: 10.5 min                                 4min + 30s = 4.5min
                                                  (+ parallel 3min)
                                                  Total: ~6 min (40% faster)

  DEPENDENCY RULES:
  ✅ Parallel: stages with NO shared output dependency
  ❌ Sequential: Stage B needs Stage A's output (build artifact, test results)

For anihpj: The GitHub Actions workflow uses needs: to express the dependency graph. Lint and unit tests run first (parallel, both need: checkout). When both pass, integration tests and Docker build run in parallel (both need: lint + unit-tests). When build succeeds, Trivy scan + Cosign sign run. When all pass, push to ECR. This cut pipeline time from 10.5 to ~6 minutes. Options A and C remove quality gates — never skip checks to save time. Option B is still sequential — just in one job.

Continuous Delivery Q116 of 200
The anihpj CI pipeline installs Python dependencies (pip install -r requirements.txt) and builds a Docker image on every run. Installing 200+ packages takes 2 minutes every time. How can the platform team dramatically speed this up?
A) CI caching — store the pip cache and Docker build layers between pipeline runs. In GitHub Actions: actions/cache@v3 with a key based on requirements.txt hash saves and restores the ~/.cache/pip directory. For Docker: use docker/build-push-action with GitHub Actions cache or registry cache — unchanged layers are reused, only changed layers are rebuilt. First run: 2 min. Subsequent runs (no dependency changes): ~10 seconds. Docker layers are also cached — base image and pip install layer only rebuild when Dockerfile or requirements change
B) Reduce the number of dependencies — remove unnecessary libraries from requirements.txt. Fewer packages means faster installation, and it also reduces the attack surface from vulnerable transitive dependencies
C) Pre-build a base Docker image with all dependencies installed and push it to ECR — the CI pipeline just pulls the pre-built image instead of installing dependencies every time. Update the base image weekly
D) Use a faster package manager like Poetry or PDM instead of pip — these tools have better dependency resolution algorithms and parallel download support, reducing install time by 50%
✅ Correct Answer: A

CI caching is the standard technique for speeding up pipelines by persisting build artifacts between runs. The insight: dependencies change MUCH less frequently than application code. By caching the dependency installation directory and Docker build layers, subsequent pipeline runs only rebuild what actually changed. GitHub Actions provides actions/cache which stores files in GitHub's blob storage (up to 10GB per repo). The cache key is typically a hash of the dependency file — when requirements.txt changes, the cache misses and dependencies are re-installed fresh. For Docker, BuildKit's layer caching is even more efficient — unchanged layers are loaded from cache instantly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CI Caching — Speed Without Sacrificing Correctness
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              CI CACHING STRATEGY FOR ANIHPJ                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CACHE #1: Python Dependencies (pip)                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  - name: Cache pip dependencies                           │   │
  │  │    uses: actions/cache@v3                                 │   │
  │  │    with:                                                  │   │
  │  │      path: ~/.cache/pip          ← What to cache          │   │
  │  │      key: pip-${{ hashFiles('requirements.txt') }}       │   │
  │  │                          ↑ Cache key = hash of deps file  │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  HOW IT WORKS:                                            │   │
  │  │  Run 1: hash reqs.txt → cache miss → pip install → save  │   │
  │  │  Run 2: hash reqs.txt → cache HIT  → skip install → ✅  │   │
  │  │  Run 3: reqs.txt changed → new hash → cache miss → redo  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CACHE #2: Docker Build Layers (BuildKit)                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Dockerfile (order matters for caching!):                  │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  FROM python:3.12-slim          ← Cached (rarely changes) │   │
  │  │  WORKDIR /app                    ← Cached                  │   │
  │  │  COPY requirements.txt .         ← Cache breaks if file   │   │
  │  │  RUN pip install -r requirements.txt ← changes           │   │
  │  │  COPY . .                        ← ALWAYS changes         │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  Build 1: All layers built (3 min)                        │   │
  │  │  Build 2: Only COPY . . rebuilt (5 sec — everything      │   │
  │  │           above was cached!)                               │   │
  │  │  Build 3: requirements.txt changed → pip install + COPY   │   │
  │  │           rebuilt (1 min — base image still cached)       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESULT:                                                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────┬──────────────────┐         │
  │  │ Scenario            │ No Cache  │ With Cache       │         │
  │  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┤         │
  │  │ No deps change      │ 5 min     │ 30 seconds  ⚡   │         │
  │  │ Deps changed        │ 5 min     │ 90 seconds  ⚡   │         │
  │  │ Dockerfile changed  │ 5 min     │ 60 seconds  ⚡   │         │
  │  └─────────────────────┴───────────┴──────────────────┘         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: pip cache + Docker BuildKit layer caching reduces average pipeline time from 8 minutes to ~2 minutes when only application code changes (which is 90% of pushes). Option B is good hygiene but not the primary speed technique. Option C is a variant of caching (base image = cache) but adds management overhead. Option D may help marginally but caching provides orders-of-magnitude improvement.

Continuous Delivery Q117 of 200
After a successful CI run, what should the pipeline produce as its "deliverables" — the outputs that downstream systems (CD, security, compliance) consume?
A) A "build passed" Slack notification — notifying the team that CI succeeded is sufficient; the Docker image is already in the registry and that's the only artifact needed
B) The CI pipeline log file — a complete log of all pipeline stages serves as the audit trail for compliance and is the primary artifact consumed by downstream systems
C) Multiple artifacts for different consumers: (1) Container image pushed to registry (ECR) — consumed by ArgoCD for deployment, (2) SBOM in SPDX format attached to image — consumed by Dependency-Track for vulnerability correlation, (3) Signed attestation (Cosign) — consumed by Kyverno for admission verification, (4) Test report (JUnit XML) — consumed by dashboard for quality metrics, (5) Image tag written to GitOps repo — triggers ArgoCD sync. Each artifact serves a different downstream system in the platform ecosystem
D) A Docker image tarball saved as a GitHub Actions artifact — the image is stored as a .tar file in GitHub's artifact storage, and CD tools download and load it into their local Docker daemon for deployment
✅ Correct Answer: C

CI pipelines produce multiple artifacts, each consumed by different downstream systems. The container image is the primary artifact (deployable unit). But modern platform engineering requires more: SBOMs for vulnerability management (Dependency-Track), signed attestations for admission control (Kyverno), test reports for quality dashboards (Grafana), and Git updates for GitOps reconciliation (ArgoCD). Each artifact serves a specific purpose in the platform ecosystem and enables automation of what would otherwise be manual processes.

For anihpj: The pipeline generates all 5 artifact types. The image goes to ECR. The SBOM goes to Dependency-Track. The attestation is verified by Kyverno at deploy time. The test report populates the quality dashboard. The Git update triggers ArgoCD to sync staging. Option A ignores all non-image artifacts. Option B treats logs as artifacts — they're for debugging, not machine consumption. Option D uses tarballs instead of a registry — this doesn't scale to CD.

Continuous Delivery Q118 of 200
The anihpj CI pipeline fails at 2 AM due to a new CVE in a Python dependency. The on-call developer needs to know immediately — not discover it 8 hours later when they check their email. How should the platform team configure pipeline notifications?
A) Email all developers on every pipeline event — CI status emails ensure everyone is always informed, and developers can set up email filters to manage the volume
B) Targeted notifications via chat platform — CI sends Slack/Discord/Teams messages to a #anihpj-builds channel. Successful builds post a brief green message. Failed builds post a red message with: the commit author (@mention), the failed stage, a link to the logs, and the error summary. This follows the "notify the person who can fix it" principle — the commit author gets immediate, actionable feedback. Nightly scan failures also notify the security team channel. No email — chat is real-time and searchable
C) Log all pipeline results to a file and check them during the daily standup — a structured daily review of CI results is more disciplined and prevents alert fatigue from real-time notifications
D) Only notify on production deployment failures — pipeline failures in dev/staging are expected during normal development and don't warrant real-time alerts; only production issues need immediate attention
✅ Correct Answer: B

Chat-based notifications are the standard for CI/CD pipelines because they are real-time (unlike email), contextual (unlike dashboards you must check), and targeted (notify the right person). The pattern: green for success, red for failure, @mention the commit author. This follows the DevOps principle of fast feedback — the developer who pushed the code that broke the build should know within minutes. Nightly security scan failures notify the security team channel separately. The notification includes: which repo/branch, which stage failed, a link to the logs, and the last few log lines so the developer can assess severity without clicking through.

For anihpj: GitHub Actions uses the slackapi/slack-github-action to post to #anihpj-builds. Failures @mention the commit author extracted from github.event.head_commit.author.username. Nightly Trivy scans post to #anihpj-security if CRITICAL CVEs found. PR checks show status in GitHub's PR UI — no notification needed. Option A creates alert fatigue. Option C delays feedback by up to 24 hours. Option D ignores non-prod failures that indicate real problems.

Continuous Delivery Q119 of 200
A developer opens a PR to the anihpj repo. How does the CI system know to run the pipeline, and how does the PR show whether checks passed or failed — all without the developer manually triggering anything?
A) The developer runs git ci-trigger after pushing their branch — a git hook sends a custom event to the CI server which then starts the pipeline manually
B) The CI system polls the git repository every 60 seconds looking for new commits — when it finds a new commit, it runs the pipeline. This polling model is how all CI systems integrate with version control
C) The developer fills out a CI request form in Backstage after opening the PR — the platform portal triggers the pipeline via a webhook to the CI system. This gives developers control over when CI runs
D) Git webhooks + Status API — when a PR is opened or a push occurs, the Git platform (GitHub/GitLab) sends an HTTP POST webhook to the CI system with the event payload (repo, branch, commit SHA, PR number). The CI system runs the pipeline and reports results back via the Commit Status API — setting a green checkmark ✅ or red X ❌ on the commit/PR. This is fully automated: no polling, no manual triggers, no forms. The developer sees pipeline status directly in the PR UI within seconds of pushing
✅ Correct Answer: D

Webhooks + Status API is the standard integration pattern between Git platforms and CI systems. GitHub (for example) sends a webhook event to a configured URL whenever specific events occur (push, pull_request, etc.). The CI system receives the event, extracts the context (repo, branch, SHA), and starts the pipeline. When the pipeline completes, the CI system calls the Commit Status API to set the check status on the commit — this is what powers the green checkmark or red X in the PR UI. This is a push-based event-driven model — no polling, no manual steps.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Git + CI Integration — Webhooks and Status API
