For anihpj: anihpj-cli is a Python CLI (Click/Typer) distributed via Homebrew and pip. It's the terminal companion to Backstage. Developers choose their interface: Backstage for visual tasks, CLI for automation and scripts. Both call the same Platform API underneath.

IDPs & DevEx Q171 of 200
The anihpj platform has 47 services across 3 teams. Developers ask: "What services exist? Who owns them? What's their health status?" What platform capability provides this visibility?
A) kubectl get all — listing all resources in all namespaces shows every service, deployment, and pod; this is the standard way to discover services in a K8s cluster
B) Service Catalog — a centralized registry of all services, APIs, and resources in the platform. In Backstage, each service is a catalog entity (kind: Component) with metadata (name, description, owner team, lifecycle stage), spec (type: service/website/library), and relations (depends on anihpj-db, owned by team-anihpj). The catalog answers: What services exist? (47 Components), Who owns this? (owner: team-anihpj), Is it healthy? (ArgoCD health status integrated), What APIs does it expose? (API docs linked), What's its deployment history? (ArgoCD sync history). This is the "single pane of glass" for the entire platform
C) A Confluence wiki page listing all services — each team maintains a wiki page for their services; the wiki is the source of truth for service ownership and status
D) Prometheus target discovery — Prometheus auto-discovers services via ServiceMonitors; the Prometheus targets page shows all services with their scrape status, which serves as a service catalog
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Service Catalog is the inventory system for your platform. It's more than a list of services — it's a graph of entities (Components, APIs, Resources, Systems, Domains) with relationships (depends on, owned by, part of). The catalog enables: discoverability (find any service in seconds), ownership (who do I ask about this service?), dependency mapping (what breaks if this service goes down?), and automation (every service in the catalog gets CI/CD, monitoring, docs).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Service Catalog — Entity Model
  catalog-info.yaml (in each service repo):
  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Component
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-api
    description: Job board REST API
    tags: [python, django, rest]
    annotations:
      github.com/project-slug: anihpj/anihpj-api
      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
  spec:
    type: service
    lifecycle: production
    owner: team-anihpj
    system: job-board
    dependsOn: [component:anihpj-db, resource:anihpj-redis]
    providesApis: [anihpj-api-v1]

For anihpj: Every service has a catalog-info.yaml in its repo. Backstage GitHub Discovery auto-registers new services. The catalog shows 47 Components with real-time health from ArgoCD and ownership from CODEOWNERS.

IDPs & DevEx Q172 of 200
A new team creates a service and pushes catalog-info.yaml to GitHub. How does it appear in Backstage — without anyone manually registering it?
A) Catalog Discovery (auto-registration) — Backstage's GitHub Discovery plugin periodically scans configured GitHub organizations/repos for catalog-info.yaml files. When it finds one, it automatically registers the entity in the catalog. Configuration: specify org (anihpj), schedule (every 5 minutes), and filter (repos containing catalog-info.yaml). This is pull-based discovery — repos don't push to Backstage; Backstage pulls from GitHub. Alternatively: manual registration (paste a URL to catalog-info.yaml), API registration (POST to /api/catalog/locations), or CI-driven (pipeline calls Backstage API on repo creation)
B) Manual registration — the developer opens Backstage, clicks "Register Entity," pastes the URL to catalog-info.yaml, and submits. This is the only way to add services to the catalog
C) Webhook from GitHub — GitHub sends a webhook to Backstage when a new repo is created, and Backstage auto-registers it. This push-based model is the most common pattern
D) kubectl register — developers run a kubectl command that creates a Backstage catalog entry as a K8s CRD; Backstage watches for these CRDs and populates the catalog
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage's discovery model is pull-based: integrations scan external systems (GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket) on a schedule, find catalog-info.yaml files, and register entities. This means there's no setup on the repo side beyond adding a catalog-info.yaml — Backstage discovers it automatically within minutes. Multiple discovery methods coexist: GitHub Discovery (scan repos), GitLab Discovery, Bitbucket Discovery, and custom Entity Providers for any system with an API.

  Developer pushes catalog-info.yaml to repo
       │

  • Scans anihpj org repos
  • Finds new catalog-info.yaml in anihpj-payments

  • Auto-registers in catalog
       │
       ▼
  Catalog updated: 48 Components (was 47)
  anihpj-payments now visible in Backstage with health, docs, ownership

For anihpj: GitHub Discovery scans the anihpj org every 5 minutes. Any repo with catalog-info.yaml is auto-registered. New services appear in Backstage without manual steps. The catalog stays in sync automatically.

D) Catalog-Driven Development — the catalog is the trigger for platform automation. Catalog-Driven Development makes the catalog the central nervous system of the platform. The catalog isn't just a directory — it's the source of truth that drives automation. IDPs & DevEx Q176 of 200
The anihpj platform team built a great IDP, but only 30% of teams use it. The rest bypass the platform and deploy directly to AWS. How should the platform team drive adoption — without using mandates?
A) Mandate platform usage via policy — create a Kyverno policy that rejects deployments not originating from the platform pipeline; enforce adoption through technical controls
B) Executive mandate — have the CTO send an email requiring all teams to migrate to the platform within 90 days; create a compliance dashboard tracking migration progress
C) Make the right way the EASY way — the platform should be so much better than DIY that teams CHOOSE it. Strategies: (1) Dogfooding — platform team uses their own platform, proving it works, (2) Golden paths that are 10x faster than DIY (5 min vs 3 days), (3) Developer Experience surveys — measure NPS and fix pain points, (4) Internal advocacy — showcase teams that adopted and their results ("Team X deploys 5x/day after adopting"), (5) Gradual migration — don't force, but stop maintaining legacy tooling ("we no longer support manual Terraform setups"). The principle: adoption through attraction, not enforcement. If you need a mandate, your platform isn't good enough yet
D) Financial incentives — offer budget increases to teams that adopt the platform and reduce funding for teams that don't; use financial levers to drive behavior change
✅ Correct Answer: C

Platform adoption is fundamentally a product problem, not an enforcement problem. The platform is a PRODUCT whose users are internal developers. If developers don't use it, the product isn't good enough — either it's too hard to use, too slow, doesn't solve real problems, or the value isn't communicated. Strategies: dogfooding builds trust ("we use it ourselves"), golden paths make it faster, DevEx surveys identify pain points, and success stories create FOMO (fear of missing out).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Platform Adoption — Attraction, Not Enforcement
  ADOPTION FUNNEL:
  1. AWARENESS: "There's a platform?" → Internal marketing, demo days
  2. INTEREST: "Can it help me?" → Success stories, metrics from early adopters
  3. TRIAL: "Let me try it" → Golden paths, easy onboarding, 5-min first deploy
  4. ADOPTION: "This is better" → Faster deploys, fewer incidents, less toil
  5. ADVOCACY: "You should use this" → Teams tell other teams
  
  KEY METRICS:
  • Adoption rate: % of services using platform
  • Self-service rate: % of operations done without tickets
  • NPS: "Would you recommend the platform to another team?"
  • Time-to-first-deploy: How fast can a new service go live?

For anihpj: 87% adoption achieved through golden paths (5 min deploy), dogfooding (platform team uses it), and continuous DevEx improvement (quarterly surveys drive roadmap). The remaining 13% are specialized teams with unique requirements — they're not forced to migrate.

5.3 Developer Portals for Platform AdoptionQ177-Q180

IDPs & DevEx Q177 of 200
Prometheus detects an anihpj-api latency spike at 3 AM. Instead of a human investigating from scratch, what AI/ML approach can assist with automated anomaly detection and root cause analysis?
A) A static threshold alert — if latency exceeds 500ms, send an alert. This is the standard approach and does not require AI/ML
B) AIOps (AI for IT Operations) — ML models analyze historical metrics to establish dynamic baselines ("normal latency for this time of day is 100-200ms"). Anomaly detection: the model detects a 400ms spike (2x baseline) and alerts with context: "Latency spike correlates with DB query time increase; recent deploy 5 minutes ago." Root cause analysis: ML correlates the latency spike with other signals — DB query time also spiked, a deployment happened 5 minutes before, error logs show connection pool exhaustion → suggests "likely caused by deployment #1247; consider rolling back." AIOps augments, not replaces, human operators — it reduces MTTR by providing faster diagnosis
C) A chatbot that answers "what's wrong?" — developers type questions into Slack, and an AI bot responds with suggestions from the runbook; this is the primary AIOps use case
D) Full automation — AI detects the issue, rolls back the deployment, scales up resources, and closes the incident without any human involvement; this is the goal of AIOps and is achievable today
✅ Correct Answer: B

AIOps applies ML to platform operations: anomaly detection (dynamic baselines instead of static thresholds), event correlation (connect related alerts — "latency spike + DB error + deploy 5 min ago"), root cause analysis (suggest the most likely cause from historical patterns), and automated runbooks (if the pattern matches a known issue, suggest or execute the fix). AIOps doesn't replace human operators — it gives them superpowers by reducing the time from "something's wrong" to "here's what's probably wrong and how to fix it."

📝 Detailed Explanation
AIOps — AI-Assisted Incident Response
  TRADITIONAL:                          AIOps-ASSISTED:
  Alert fires → Human investigates      Alert fires → ML analyzes context
  (30 min to find root cause)           → "Latency spike correlates with:
                                           DB query time +200%,
                                           Deploy #1247 5 min ago,
                                           Connection pool exhausted"
                                        → Human decides: Rollback deploy
                                        (5 min to diagnose + decide)
  
  AIOps doesn't replace humans — it reduces MTTR by 6x

For anihpj: Prometheus metrics feed an anomaly detection model. When the anihpj-api latency spikes, the model compares against historical patterns and correlates with recent deploys, DB metrics, and error logs. The on-call engineer gets a Slack message: "Likely caused by deploy #1247 (DB connection pool config change)."

IDPs & DevEx Q179 of 200
The anihpj platform handles 1,200 deploys/month. Traffic spikes every Friday at 5 PM. Instead of manually scaling before the spike, how can ML help predict and automate scaling?
A) Predictive Scaling — ML models analyze historical traffic patterns (time of day, day of week, seasonal trends) to predict future load. The model learns: "every Friday 4:30-6:30 PM, traffic increases 3x." It triggers a pre-scale at 4:15 PM — increasing replicas from 5 to 15 BEFORE the traffic arrives. Traditional reactive scaling (HPA) waits for CPU to spike, then scales — causing a brief degradation. Predictive scaling prevents the degradation entirely. This requires: historical metrics (Prometheus), ML training pipeline, and a custom controller that adjusts replica counts based on predictions
B) HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) — the standard K8s autoscaler that increases replicas when CPU/memory exceeds targets; it handles Friday spikes by reacting to the increased load within 30 seconds, which is sufficient
C) CronJob scaling — a scheduled CronJob that runs at 4 PM every Friday and runs kubectl scale to increase replicas; this is simpler than ML and achieves the same result for predictable patterns
D) Manual scaling — the on-call engineer sets a calendar reminder to scale up before known traffic spikes; this is the most reliable approach because humans understand context better than ML models
✅ Correct Answer: A

Predictive scaling uses ML to anticipate load rather than react to it. While HPA (Option B) reacts, predictive scaling acts BEFORE the spike — eliminating the brief degradation during HPA's scale-up window. CronJobs (Option C) work for fixed schedules but can't adapt to changing patterns (what if the spike shifts to Thursday?). Predictive models continuously learn and adapt.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Predictive vs Reactive Scaling
  REACTIVE (HPA):                      PREDICTIVE (ML):
  Traffic rises → CPU > 80%            ML predicts: "Friday 5pm = 3x traffic"
  → HPA scales: 5→10→15              → Pre-scale at 4:15pm: 5→15
  → Users see brief slowdown ✓         → Traffic arrives at 5pm → 15 pods ready ✓
  (lag: 1-3 minutes)                   (lag: 0 — scaled BEFORE spike)
  
  Predictive scaling = HPA + ML predictions + pre-scaling controller
  ML model continuously retrains from latest traffic patterns

For anihpj: A Python ML model (Prophet/ARIMA) analyzes 6 months of traffic data. Predicts Friday 5pm spikes. A custom controller pre-scales anihpj-api at 4:15pm. Combined with HPA as fallback (if prediction is wrong, HPA still catches it).

IDPs & DevEx Q180 of 200
An alert fires, and an AI analyzes the context, suggests a fix, and offers to auto-remediate. How does AI assist in incident response without removing human judgment?
A) AI auto-remediates all incidents without human approval — if the AI is confident enough (based on historical accuracy), it applies the fix automatically and only notifies humans after the fact
B) AI replaces the on-call rotation — instead of humans being paged, AI handles all incident response 24/7; humans are only involved for postmortems and process improvement
C) AI generates a postmortem document after the incident is resolved — it analyzes the timeline, Slack conversations, and logs to auto-generate a blameless postmortem for human review
D) AI-assisted triage with human decision — the AI analyzes the alert, correlates with recent changes, checks logs and metrics, and presents a summary to the on-call engineer: "Alert: anihpj-api error rate 12%. Likely cause: DB connection pool exhaustion (max_connections=100, current=100). Recent change: deploy #1247 reduced pool size from 200 to 100. Suggested fix: revert deploy #1247 or increase max_connections." The human decides: click "Revert Deploy" (auto-executed) or "Investigate Further." AI speeds up triage (from 30 min to 2 min) while keeping humans in control for decisions with business impact
✅ Correct Answer: D

AI-assisted incident response augments human operators: AI handles data gathering and correlation (which would take a human 30 minutes of querying dashboards, logs, and git history), and presents actionable findings. The human makes the decision — especially for changes with business impact. This is the "centaur" model: AI + human together outperform either alone. AI does what it's good at (fast data analysis); humans do what they're good at (judgment, context, accountability).

📝 Detailed Explanation
AI-Assisted Incident Response — Human + AI Centaur Model
  ALERT FIRES (2 AM)
       │
       ▼
  AI ANALYZES (5 seconds):
  • Checks recent deploys → deploy #1247 changed DB pool config
  • Queries metrics → DB connections saturated at 100/100
  • Reads logs → "FATAL: too many connections"
  • Suggests: "Revert deploy #1247"
       │
       ▼
  HUMAN DECIDES (30 seconds):
  • Reviews AI's analysis
  • Clicks "Revert"
       │
       ▼
  AUTO-EXECUTE: Rollback deploys, verifies health
  Incident resolved in 2 minutes (vs 30+ minutes without AI)

For anihpj: The incident response pipeline uses AI for initial triage. The on-call engineer reviews and decides. Low-risk fixes (restart pod, scale up) are auto-approved. High-risk fixes (rollback, schema change reversion) require human approval.

6.1 Platform Efficiency & Team ProductivityQ185-Q192

Measuring Platform Q185 of 200
Before the anihpj platform: 4 hours to deploy a new service. After platform (Backstage golden path): 5 minutes. What metrics capture the platform's efficiency impact?
A) Platform efficiency metrics: time-to-first-deploy (4h→5min = 98% reduction), deploy frequency (1/week→5/day = 35x increase), toil hours saved (500h/month automated), cost per deployment (down 90% from manual ops), and self-service rate (87% of operations done without tickets). These metrics quantify the platform's ROI and justify continued investment. Combined with DORA metrics (delivery performance) and DevEx metrics (developer satisfaction), they provide a complete picture of platform impact
B) Only uptime matters — if the platform is available 99.9% of the time, it's successful; efficiency metrics are secondary and don't impact user experience
C) Revenue impact — the only metric that matters is whether the platform increases company revenue; technical metrics are internal and don't matter to the business
D) Developer headcount — if the number of developers is growing, the platform is successful; headcount is the simplest and most reliable metric
✅ Correct Answer: A

Platform efficiency metrics tell the ROI story. The 98% reduction in time-to-deploy translates directly to engineering hours saved (500h/month) and faster time-to-market. Combined with DORA (delivery performance) and DevEx (satisfaction), these metrics justify platform investment and guide continuous improvement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Platform Efficiency Metrics — Before vs After
  METRIC                    BEFORE         AFTER          IMPROVEMENT
  Time-to-first-deploy      4 hours        5 minutes      98% reduction
  Deploy frequency          1/week         5/day          35x increase
  Toil (manual ops)         500h/month     50h/month      90% reduction
  Cost per deploy           $50            $5             90% reduction
  Self-service rate         10%            87%            8.7x increase

For anihpj: Monthly platform review dashboard shows all efficiency metrics. The 500h/month savings justified hiring 2 additional platform engineers — the platform pays for itself.

Measuring Platform Q186 of 200
The anihpj platform team conducts quarterly developer surveys: "How easy is it to deploy a new service?" (1-10) and "Would you recommend the platform?" (NPS). What framework structures these satisfaction measurements?
A) SLA reporting — uptime and latency commitments to developers; this is the primary satisfaction metric
B) HR performance reviews — developer ratings of the platform team in quarterly reviews
C) NPS + SPACE framework: NPS (Net Promoter Score) — "How likely to recommend?" (0-10), Promoters (9-10) minus Detractors (0-6) = NPS. Target > 50. SPACE: Satisfaction (surveys), Performance (DORA), Activity (deploy count), Communication (docs quality), Efficiency (time-to-first-deploy). Together = 360° developer experience measurement. NPS identifies sentiment; SPACE identifies which dimension to improve
D) GitHub stars — internal repo stars indicate developer satisfaction; more stars = happier developers
✅ Correct Answer: C

NPS + SPACE are the standard DevEx frameworks. NPS provides a benchmarkable satisfaction score. SPACE provides a holistic view across 5 dimensions. Together they answer: "Are developers happy?" (NPS) and "What should we improve?" (SPACE dimension scores).

📝 Detailed Explanation
SPACE Framework — 5 Dimensions of Developer Productivity
  S - Satisfaction: NPS surveys, ease-of-use ratings
  P - Performance: DORA (deploy freq, lead time, MTTR, CFR)
  A - Activity: Deploys/month, PRs merged, features shipped
  C - Communication: Docs quality, search discoverability, onboarding speed
  E - Efficiency: Time-to-first-deploy, toil reduction, self-service rate
  
  For anihpj: NPS = 62 (up from 12). Top SPACE gap: "Efficiency" (staging slow). Fixed.

For anihpj: Quarterly SPACE survey. NPS = 62. When it dropped to 35 (staging too slow), pipeline parallelization brought it back. Data-driven platform improvement.

Measuring Platform Q187 of 200
The anihpj platform has 45 services, 1,200 deploys/month, and 87% self-service rate. The platform team wants to track adoption growth. What metrics measure platform adoption?
A) Only the total number of services — if the count is growing, adoption is increasing
B) Adoption metrics: % of services on platform (45/52 = 87%), monthly active services (deployed at least once), self-service rate (% of operations without tickets — 87%), team adoption (% of teams using platform), golden path usage (% of new services started via scaffolder), and time-to-onboard (new team to first production deploy). Track TRENDS: is adoption growing, flat, or declining? A flat adoption rate despite new teams joining = the platform isn't attracting new users — investigate why
C) Number of Backstage page views — if developers are visiting the portal, they're adopting the platform; page views are the simplest adoption proxy
D) Mandate compliance — if all teams are using the platform because it's required, adoption is 100% by definition
✅ Correct Answer: B

Adoption metrics measure how deeply the platform is embedded in the organization. Beyond just counting services, you need to track: what % of eligible services are on the platform, how many operations are self-service vs ticket-driven, and whether new teams choose the platform. Trends matter more than absolute numbers — a flat adoption rate signals a problem.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Platform Adoption Dashboard
  Services on platform: 45/52 (87%) ↑ from 78% last quarter
  Self-service rate: 87% ↑ from 72%
  Teams adopted: 8/10 ↑ from 6/10
  Golden path usage: 73% of new services
  
  Key insight: 87% self-service means only 13% of operations need platform team tickets

For anihpj: Monthly adoption review. Current: 87% adoption (up from 78%). Goal: 95%. Strategy: showcase success stories, improve golden paths, reduce friction for the remaining 13%.

Measuring Platform Q188 of 200
Before the platform: 20 hours/week of manual operations (DNS setup, TLS certs, DB provisioning, log collection). After: 2 hours/week. What metric captures the reduction of repetitive manual work?
A) Deploy frequency — how often code is deployed to production; this measures throughput, not manual work reduction
B) MTTR — how long to recover from incidents; this measures resilience, not ongoing operational toil
C) Resource utilization — CPU and memory usage of the platform infrastructure; this measures efficiency of compute resources, not human effort
D) Toil reduction — measuring and eliminating repetitive, manual, automatable work. Toil = operational tasks that are manual, repetitive, automatable, tactical (no enduring value), and scale linearly with service growth. Before platform: 20h/week manual ops. After platform (automated DNS, TLS, DB, logs): 2h/week — 90% reduction. This frees engineers to work on improvements instead of keeping lights on. SRE principle: keep toil below 50% of engineering time
✅ Correct Answer: D

Toil reduction is one of the strongest platform ROI metrics. Toil (a Site Reliability Engineering concept) is work that's manual, repetitive, automatable, and doesn't provide enduring value. Every hour of toil eliminated is an hour returned to engineering. The platform's automation (cert-manager for TLS, Crossplane for DB provisioning, ArgoCD for deployments, ESO for secrets) directly eliminates toil.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Toil Reduction — Before vs After Platform
  BEFORE (20h/week manual ops):
  DNS setup: 2h | TLS certs: 3h | DB provisioning: 5h
  Log collection: 3h | Secret rotation: 2h | Manual deploys: 5h
  
  AFTER (2h/week, all automated):
  cert-manager (auto TLS), Crossplane (auto DB), ESO (auto secrets)
  ArgoCD (auto deploy), ExternalDNS (auto DNS), Loki (auto logs)
  
  TOIL REDUCED: 90% (18h/week saved → 936h/year returned to engineering)

For anihpj: Toil tracking is part of the monthly platform review. 90% toil reduction was the key metric used to justify the platform team's budget and headcount. Engineers now spend time on improvements, not manual ops.

Measuring Platform Q189 of 200
The anihpj platform costs $5,000/month in cloud resources. The platform team uses Kubecost to analyze spend. How does cost efficiency measurement improve platform operations?
A) Cost doesn't matter — platform engineering is about developer velocity; cloud costs are the finance team's responsibility, not the platform team's
B) FinOps with Kubecost — breaks down costs by namespace, team, service, and resource type. Reveals: anihpj-ml is 40% of spend but only 5% of traffic → right-size or use spot instances. Idle resources: 30% of CPU allocated but unused → reduce requests. Recommendations: save $1,200/month by right-sizing and using spot instances. Cost efficiency is part of platform responsibility — efficient platforms are sustainable platforms. Developers get cost dashboards showing their team's spend → creates cost awareness without blocking velocity
C) Minimize costs at all costs — use the smallest instances, fewest replicas, and cheapest regions regardless of performance impact; cost optimization is the #1 priority
D) Charge teams per API call — implement a billing system where teams pay for platform usage per request; this market-based approach naturally optimizes costs
✅ Correct Answer: B

FinOps brings financial accountability to cloud spending. Kubecost shows: cost per namespace, per team, per service. Right-sizing recommendations identify over-provisioned resources. Spot instance recommendations for fault-tolerant workloads. Cost dashboards give teams visibility into their spend — creating awareness without blocking velocity. The goal is NOT to minimize cost — it's to optimize cost while maintaining performance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubecost — Cloud Cost Visibility
  anihpj monthly spend: $5,000
  Breakdown by service: anihpj-api $800, anihpj-db $1,200, anihpj-ml $2,000...
  Right-sizing savings: $300/month (reduce over-provisioned CPU)
  Spot instance savings: $900/month (ML workloads on spot)
  Total potential savings: $1,200/month (24% reduction)

For anihpj: Kubecost dashboards in Backstage show per-team costs. ML team saw they were 40% of spend → switched to spot instances → saved $900/month. Platform team reviews costs monthly alongside other metrics.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Troubleshooting Issues

Real-world Kubernetes failure scenarios — each with symptom, 15 categorized causes (5 most likely + 5 less likely + 5 new cluster), diagnostic commands, solutions, and personal advice. Patterned after the 5+5+5 cause-card format.

100Issues
5Categories
15Causes per Issue

💾 Category 1: Storage Troubleshooting (10 Issues)ST1-ST10

🟢 Storage Issues — PVC, PV, StorageClass, and Mount Failures

Storage is often the FIRST thing to break because it involves multiple components: PVC → StorageClass → Provisioner → PV → Mount. When any link in this chain fails, your pod can't start or your data is at risk.

📌 ST1–ST3: PVC Binding Failures

ST1
💾 CATEGORY 1: STORAGE — Issue ST1
PVC Stuck in Pending State — No PV Bound

🔍 Symptom: kubectl get pvc shows STATUS: Pending indefinitely. kubectl describe pvc shows "no persistent volumes available for this claim." The anihpj-db PostgreSQL pod can't start because its PVC won't bind.

  kubectl get pvc
  NAME         STATUS    VOLUME   CAPACITY   STORAGECLASS   AGE
  anihpj-pvc   Pending                      fast-ssd       5m

  kubectl describe pvc anihpj-pvc
  Events:
    Warning  VolumeBinding  no persistent volumes available for this claim
    Warning  VolumeBinding  storageclass.storage.k8s.io "fast-ssd" not found
🔴5 Most Likely Causes
  1. StorageClass does not exist: The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created. kubectl get sc — if "fast-ssd" is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
  2. No matching PV available: In static provisioning, no PV matches the PVC's storage size, access mode, or selector. kubectl get pv — check capacity and accessModes.
  3. StorageClass provisioner not installed: The CSI driver (e.g., ebs.csi.aws.com) is not deployed. kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep csi — provisioner pods must be Running.
  4. Access mode mismatch: PVC requests ReadWriteMany but only ReadWriteOnce PVs exist. Check kubectl get pv -o yaml | grep -A2 accessModes.
  5. StorageClass has volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer and no pod uses the PVC yet. The PV is only created when a pod actually references the PVC.
🟡5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CSI driver pod CrashLoopBackOff — provisioner can't create volumes
  2. Cloud provider API rate limiting — too many PVCs created simultaneously
  3. PVC selector labels don't match any PV labels
  4. Node where CSI driver runs has taint without toleration
  5. storageClassName: "" (empty string, not null!) — requests a PV with NO StorageClass, but all PVs have one
🟣5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No default StorageClass set: kubectl get sc — look for "(default)". Without it, PVCs without storageClassName won't bind. Set: kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}'
  2. CSI driver not yet installed — common in fresh kubeadm clusters
  3. Cloud credentials not configured for CSI driver to provision volumes
  4. RBAC for CSI driver service account missing
  5. kubelet missing --cloud-provider flag for legacy in-tree provisioners
🔍 What to Look At:
kubectl get sc,pv,pvc --all-namespaces The full storage stack in one view — see StorageClass, PV, and PVC together.
kubectl describe pvc <name> The Events section tells you exactly why binding failed — always read the events first.
🎯 #1 Root Cause: StorageClass does not exist or is misnamed. The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created — the most common CKA exam pitfall.
🔧 How to Solve:
1. Verify StorageClass exists kubectl get sc If the StorageClass referenced by the PVC is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
2. Check provisioner and parameters kubectl describe sc <name> Verify the provisioner is correct and the CSI driver is installed and running.
3. Static provisioning kubectl get pv Create a PV matching the PVC's requested size, accessMode, and selector labels.
4. Dynamic provisioning Install the CSI driver: kubectl apply -f <csi-driver-url>. Verify CSI pods are Running in kube-system.
5. Set default StorageClass kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}' PVCs without an explicit storageClassName will use the default.
💡 Personal Advice:
Always verify StorageClass FIRST. The #1 PVC issue on the CKA exam is a missing or misnamed StorageClass. Run kubectl get sc before writing any PVC YAML.
Empty string is NOT null. storageClassName: "" Requests a PV with NO StorageClass — different from omitting the field entirely. Know this distinction.
CSI driver must be Running. Even with correct StorageClass, if the CSI provisioner pod is CrashLoopBackOff, no volumes will be created.
WaitForFirstConsumer delays binding. If volumeBindingMode is WaitForFirstConsumer, the PV is only created when a pod actually uses the PVC. Don't mistake this for a failure.

💾 Category 1: Storage Lab ScenariosS1-S10

🟢 anihpj/jobpost — Full-Stack Django + PostgreSQL Application

What is anihpj/jobpost? A Django-based job board application where employers post jobs and applicants apply. It consists of a Django web server, PostgreSQL database, and Redis cache. Each scenario deploys a part of this app with intentional bugs — your job is to find and fix them. ⚠️ IMPORTANT: Check the cluster requirement at the top of each scenario before applying!

S1
🧪 SCENARIO S1 — Category 1: Storage PVC Debug

Deploy anihpj PostgreSQL — Fix PVC Stuck in Pending

The Problem: You deploy the anihpj/jobpost PostgreSQL database using a StatefulSet with a PersistentVolumeClaim. The pod stays Pending because the PVC won't bind — the StorageClass name is misspelled as "fast-ssd" but the actual StorageClass is named "fast-ssd-sc". Your job: find the mismatch and fix it so the database starts correctly.

🖥️ CLUSTER: ksw → 1 (kubeadm) — Production-like cluster on node "anih"
1

📋 Deploy anihpj PostgreSQL (CONTAINS THE BUG)

📖 APP STORY: anihpj/jobpost is a Django job board app. Employers post jobs, applicants apply. PostgreSQL stores: users, jobs, applications. Redis caches: job listings, session data. Django serves: REST API at /api, admin at /admin. The database pod won't start because of a StorageClass typo — the entire job board is DOWN.
📁 FILE STRUCTURE for anihpj/jobpost (S1): 
anihpj/
├── 00-namespace.yaml              ← kubectl create ns anihpj
├── 01-storageclass.yaml           ← fast-ssd-sc (default, no-provisioner)
├── 02-persistentvolume.yaml       ← anihpj-db-pv (hostPath, 10Gi)
├── 03-postgres-pvc.yaml           ← PVC with BUG: storageClassName "fast-ssd"
├── 04-postgres-statefulset.yaml   ← StatefulSet with BUG in volumeClaimTemplates
└── 05-postgres-service.yaml       ← ClusterIP Service (port 5432)
BASH — Create the StorageClass + Deploy PostgreSQL with bug 
# ============================================
# SCENARIO S1: PVC Stuck in Pending — StorageClass Mismatch
# ============================================

# 1. CREATE NAMESPACE
kubectl create namespace anihpj

# 2. CREATE THE STORAGECLASS (correct name: fast-ssd-sc)
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
# ├── 03-postgres-pvc.yaml          ← PVC with BUG: storageClassName "fast-ssd"
# ├── 04-postgres-statefulset.yaml  ← StatefulSet with BUG in volumeClaimTemplates
# └── 05-postgres-service.yaml      ← ClusterIP Service (port 5432)
# ============================================

# 1. CREATE NAMESPACE
kubectl create namespace anihpj

# 2. CREATE THE STORAGECLASS (correct name: fast-ssd-sc)
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: storage.k8s.io/v1
kind: StorageClass
metadata:
  name: fast-ssd-sc
  annotations:
    storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class: "true"
provisioner: kubernetes.io/no-provisioner
volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer
EOF

# 3. CREATE THE PV (static provisioning)
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolume
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db-pv
spec:
  capacity:
    storage: 10Gi
  accessModes:
  - ReadWriteOnce
  persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain
  storageClassName: fast-ssd-sc
  hostPath:
    path: /mnt/data/anihpj-db
EOF

# 4. DEPLOY PVC + STATEFULSET (BUG: storageClassName: "fast-ssd")
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db-data
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  accessModes:
  - ReadWriteOnce
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 10Gi
  storageClassName: fast-ssd    # ❌ BUG! Should be "fast-ssd-sc"
---
# StatefulSet — PostgreSQL Database for anihpj/jobpost
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  serviceName: anihpj-db
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj-db
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj-db
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: postgres
        image: postgres:15-alpine
        env:
        - name: POSTGRES_DB
          value: jobpost
        - name: POSTGRES_USER
          value: anihpj
        - name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
          value: secret123
        ports:
        - containerPort: 5432
        volumeMounts:
        - name: db-data
          mountPath: /var/lib/postgresql/data
  volumeClaimTemplates:
  - metadata:
      name: db-data
    spec:
      accessModes:
      - ReadWriteOnce
      resources:
        requests:
          storage: 10Gi
      storageClassName: fast-ssd  # ❌ BUG! Same typo here
---
# Service — ClusterIP for PostgreSQL (port 5432)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: anihpj-db
  ports:
  - port: 5432
    targetPort: 5432
EOF
⚠

⚠️ Observe the Error — Spot What's Broken

✗1. Check pods: kubectl get pods -n anihpj
✗2. Check PVC: kubectl get pvc -n anihpj → STATUS: Pending
✗3. Check pod events: kubectl describe pod -n anihpj anihpj-db-0 → "persistentvolumeclaim not found" or "pod has unbound PVC"
✗4. The database pod cannot start — job board is DOWN!
🔍

🔍 Debug — Find the Root Cause

1Describe the PVC: kubectl describe pvc -n anihpj anihpj-db-data
→ Events: "storageclass.storage.k8s.io 'fast-ssd' not found"
2Check available StorageClasses: kubectl get sc
→ Only "fast-ssd-sc" exists (with (default) annotation)
3Check PVC storageClassName: kubectl get pvc -n anihpj -o yaml | grep storageClassName
→ storageClassName: fast-ssd (WRONG!)
4Compare: PVC says fast-ssd — StorageClass is fast-ssd-sc
5Root cause: Typo in storageClassName — "fast-ssd" instead of "fast-ssd-sc"
🧠 Diagnostic Tenet (Thought Process)
①
Pod Pending → Check Events
②
PVC not binding → Check SC name
③
kubectl get sc → "fast-ssd-sc" exists
④
PVC says "fast-ssd" → MISMATCH!
⑤
Delete StatefulSet → Recreate → Pod starts → DB ready

Tenet: When a PVC stays Pending, the #1 cause is a StorageClass name mismatch. The StorageClass must exist with the EXACT name specified. On a StatefulSet, volumeClaimTemplates are IMMUTABLE — you must DELETE the StatefulSet and recreate it with the corrected StorageClass. Never try to patch or edit a running StatefulSet's PVC template. On the CKA exam, "PVC stuck Pending due to StorageClass typo" is one of the most common tasks.

📟 Command Outputs — ERROR State (BEFORE fix)
$ kubectl get pods -n anihpj NAME READY STATUS RESTARTS AGE anihpj-db-0 0/1 Pending 0 2m
$ kubectl describe pod anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj | tail -5 Events: Warning FailedScheduling pod has unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims Warning FailedScheduling persistentvolumeclaim "db-data-anihpj-db-0" not found
$ kubectl get pvc -n anihpj NAME STATUS VOLUME CAPACITY STORAGECLASS db-data-anihpj-db-0 Pending fast-ssd ← Wrong SC!
$ kubectl get sc NAME PROVISIONER RECLAIMPOLICY VOLUMEBINDINGMODE fast-ssd-sc (default) kubernetes.io/no-provisioner Retain WaitForFirstConsumer
📟 Command Outputs — AFTER Fix
$ kubectl get pvc -n anihpj NAME STATUS VOLUME CAPACITY STORAGECLASS db-data-anihpj-db-0 Bound anihpj-db-pv 10Gi fast-ssd-sc ✅
$ kubectl get pods -n anihpj NAME READY STATUS RESTARTS AGE anihpj-db-0 1/1 Running 0 30s
$ kubectl exec -n anihpj anihpj-db-0 -- psql -U anihpj -d jobpost -c "SELECT 1;" ?column? ---------- 1 (1 row) ✅ Database is operational!
🔧
BASH — Delete and recreate (volumeClaimTemplates are immutable) 
# Step 1: Delete the broken StatefulSet (volumeClaimTemplates are IMMUTABLE — can't edit)
# Also delete the PVCs it created so the new StatefulSet starts fresh
kubectl delete statefulset anihpj-db -n anihpj
kubectl delete pvc db-data-anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj 2>/dev/null

# Step 2: Recreate StatefulSet with CORRECT storageClassName in volumeClaimTemplates
# ⚠️ The volumeClaimTemplates auto-generates PVC named db-data-anihpj-db-0
# You do NOT need a separate PVC — the StatefulSet creates it automatically
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  serviceName: anihpj-db
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj-db
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj-db
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: postgres
        image: postgres:15-alpine
        env:
        - name: POSTGRES_DB
          value: jobpost
        - name: POSTGRES_USER
          value: anihpj
        - name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
          value: secret123
        ports:
        - containerPort: 5432
        volumeMounts:
        - name: db-data
          mountPath: /var/lib/postgresql/data
  volumeClaimTemplates:
  - metadata:
      name: db-data
    spec:
      accessModes:
      - ReadWriteOnce
      resources:
        requests:
          storage: 10Gi
      storageClassName: fast-ssd-sc  # ✅ FIXED!
---
# Service — ClusterIP for anihpj-db
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: anihpj-db
  ports:
  - port: 5432
    targetPort: 5432
EOF

# Step 3: Wait for pod to be Ready
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod/anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj --timeout=120s
✓

✅ Verify — Database Is Running

After deleting and recreating the StatefulSet with the corrected storageClassName: fast-ssd-sc, the volumeClaimTemplate auto-generates PVC db-data-anihpj-db-0. It binds to the PV, PostgreSQL initializes, and the job board database is ready. kubectl get pods -n anihpj shows anihpj-db-0 Running 1/1 and kubectl get pvc -n anihpj shows db-data-anihpj-db-0 STATUS: Bound.

💡 Key takeaway: StatefulSet volumeClaimTemplates are immutable — you cannot patch or edit them on a running StatefulSet. The only fix is to delete and recreate. The template auto-generates PVCs named <template-name>-<statefulset-name>-<ordinal>. You never need to create PVCs manually for a StatefulSet.

🧹
BASH — copy & paste to clean up 
# Delete namespace — cascades everything inside
kubectl delete namespace anihpj

# Delete the PV (not namespaced)
kubectl delete pv anihpj-db-pv

# Delete the StorageClass
kubectl delete sc fast-ssd-sc

# Verify cleanup
kubectl get all,pvc,pv,sc -n anihpj 2>/dev/null
kubectl get ns anihpj 2>/dev/null

🌳 Decision Trees — Systematic TroubleshootingDT1–DT15

Decision trees provide a step-by-step flowchart to diagnose common Kubernetes issues. Each node is a question — answer YES or NO to navigate to the next step. Follow the path to find the root cause and fix.

DT1
🌳 DECISION TREE DT1 — Pod Stuck Pending

Pod Stuck in Pending — Systematic Diagnosis Flowchart

The Problem: A pod is stuck in Pending state and won't schedule. This is the #1 most common Kubernetes issue. The scheduler can't place the pod on any node. Your job: follow this decision tree to find WHY and fix it.

🖥️ CLUSTER: ksw → 1 (kubeadm) — Any cluster
▶

🚦 START — Pod Is Pending

📖 APP STORY: The anihpj-api pod was deployed 20 minutes ago. It's still Pending — users can't access the job board. Is it resources? nodeSelector? Taints? PVC? Follow the tree to find out.
kubectl get pods -n anihpj

🌿 NODE 1 — What does kubectl describe show?

# Run this command first:
kubectl describe pod -n anihpj <pod-name> | tail -20



    
Events Section — What Does It Show?

    
    
        
  • "0/N nodes available"
    → Go to NODE 2
    • 
        
  • "pod has unbound PVC"
    → Go to NODE 3
    • 
        
  • "node(s) had taint"
    → Go to NODE 4
    • 
        
  • "insufficient cpu/memory"
    → Go to NODE 5
    • 
        
  • "didn't match selector"
    → Go to NODE 6
    • 
        
  • No events / empty
    → Check scheduler logs
    •
N2

🔴 NODE 2 — "0/N nodes are available"

                    

                        

                            

                            

                                
Check node status

                                kubectl get nodes
                            

                        

                    




    
Node Availability — Are ALL Nodes Ready?

    
    
        
  • YES → Check if node is cordoned:
    kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{.items[*].spec.unschedulable}'
    If true → Node is cordoned! Uncordon it.
    • 
        
  • NO → Some nodes NotReady → Check conditions:
    kubectl describe node <node> | grep Conditions
    MemoryPressure/DiskPressure/PIDPressure?
    • 
        
  • SSH to node — check kubelet:
    systemctl status kubelet
    journalctl -u kubelet -n 50
    • 
    






    
Restart kubelet on broken node

    kubectl uncordon <node-name>
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
N3

🔴 NODE 3 — "pod has unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims"

                    

                        

                            

                            

                                
Check PVC status

                                kubectl get pvc -n anihpj
                            

                        

                    




    
PVC Diagnosis — Why Is It Stuck?

    
    
        
  • "no persistent volumes available"?
    kubectl get pv — check if any PV matches
    StorageClass mismatch? PV capacity < PVC request? accessMode mismatch?
    • 
        
  • "storageclass not found"?
    kubectl get sc — does the SC exist?
    Provisioner broken? Check CSI driver pods
    • 
        
  • VolumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer?
    PVC stays Pending until a Pod uses it — this is NORMAL, deploy the pod first
    • 
    






    
Fix StorageClass name in PVC

    kubectl apply -f pv.yaml
kubectl patch pvc <name> -n anihpj --type=json \
-p='[{"op":"replace","path":"/spec/storageClassName","value":"correct-sc"}]'
N4

🔴 NODE 4 — "node(s) had taint that the pod didn't tolerate"

Check node taints
kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"\t"}{.spec.taints}{"\n"}{end}'
Check pod tolerations
kubectl get pod -n anihpj <pod> -o yaml | grep -A5 tolerations
Taint Fix Options
  • Option 1: Add toleration to pod spec
    Patch the Deployment to tolerate the taint
  • Option 2: Remove taint from node (if safe)
    kubectl taint node <node> <key>-
    Only do this if other workloads SHOULD run on this node
Add toleration to pod (patch deployment)
kubectl taint node <node> node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane:NoSchedule- kubectl patch deploy <name> -n anihpj --type=json -p='[ {"op":"add","path":"/spec/template/spec/tolerations","value":[{"key":"key","operator":"Equal","value":"val","effect":"NoSchedule"}]} ]'
N5

🔴 NODE 5 — "insufficient cpu/memory"

                    

                        

                            

                            

                                
Check node capacity

                                kubectl describe nodes | grep -A5 "Allocated resources"
                                kubectl top nodes
                            

                        

                    




    
Resource Fix Options

    
    
        
  • Reduce pod resource requests
    Lower CPU/memory requests to fit available capacity
    • 
        
  • Add more nodes to the cluster
    Scale horizontally if this is a persistent need
    • 
        
  • Remove old pods to free resources
    kubectl delete pod <old-pod>
    • 
        
  • Use Cluster Autoscaler (cloud only)
    • 
    






    
Lower CPU request

    kubectl patch deploy <name> -n anihpj --type=json -p='[
{"op":"replace","path":"/spec/template/spec/containers/0/resources/requests/cpu","value":"100m"}
]'
N6

🔴 NODE 6 — "didn't match Pod's node affinity/selector"

Check pod's node selector
kubectl get pod -n anihpj <pod> -o yaml | grep -A5 nodeSelector
Check pod's node affinity
kubectl get pod -n anihpj <pod> -o yaml | grep -A20 affinity
Check node labels
kubectl get nodes --show-labels
Selector/Affinity Diagnosis
  • Does any node have the required label?
    kubectl get nodes --show-labels
    If NO → Label a node: kubectl label node <node> key=value
  • Is podAntiAffinity blocking?
    Other pods with same label on that node? If maxSkew=0, odd pods ÷ zones = deadlock
    Increase maxSkew or reduce replicas
Remove impossible nodeSelector
kubectl label node <node> disktype=ssd kubectl patch deploy <name> -n anihpj --type=json -p='[ {"op":"remove","path":"/spec/template/spec/nodeSelector/disktype"} ]'
✓

✅ Decision Tree Complete — Pod Pending Resolution

Quick reference:
kubectl describe pod → Read Events → Follow the path.
N2 = Nodes not available · N3 = PVC not bound · N4 = Taints · N5 = No resources · N6 = Selector mismatch
Pro tip: 90% of Pending pods are N4 (taints) or N6 (selectors). Start there.

📋 Appendix A: Answer KeyMCQ Answers

Quick-reference answer key for all 200 multiple-choice questions across the 5 CKA categories.

Category 1: Storage (10% — Q1-Q20)
  • Q1-Q4: Storage fundamentals — emptyDir vs PV, container filesystem layers, volume/volumeMount
  • Q5-Q10: StorageClass + Dynamic Provisioning — provisioners, reclaimPolicy, WaitForFirstConsumer
  • Q11-Q15: PV/PVC Lifecycle — binding, accessModes, capacity matching, hostPath vs CSI
  • Q16-Q20: ConfigMap/Secret volumes, subPath, fsGroup, ResourceQuota for storage
Category 2: Troubleshooting (30% — Q21-Q80)
  • Q21-Q30: Node-level debugging — NotReady, MemoryPressure, DiskPressure, kubelet
  • Q31-Q40: Pod-level debugging — CrashLoopBackOff, ImagePullBackOff, OOMKilled, exit codes
  • Q41-Q50: Networking debugging — Service endpoints, DNS, NetworkPolicy, kube-proxy
  • Q51-Q60: Cluster debugging — etcd, certificates, CNI, API server, control plane
  • Q61-Q70: Application debugging — kubectl exec/logs, init containers, probes, finalizers
  • Q71-Q80: Storage debugging — PVC pending, volume mount errors, CSI driver issues
Category 3: Workloads & Scheduling (15% — Q81-Q110)
  • Q81-Q90: Deployments — RollingUpdate, Rollback, pause/resume, revision history
  • Q91-Q100: Scheduling — nodeSelector, affinity/antiAffinity, taints/tolerations, topologySpread
  • Q101-Q110: Scaling — HPA, PDB, ResourceQuota, LimitRange, Jobs, CronJobs, DaemonSets
Category 4: Services & Networking (20% — Q111-Q150)
  • Q111-Q120: Services — ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalName, EndpointSlices
  • Q121-Q130: Ingress & Gateway API — TLS, path routing, backendRef, traffic splitting
  • Q131-Q140: NetworkPolicy — ingress/egress rules, podSelector, namespaceSelector, default-deny
  • Q141-Q150: DNS & Service Discovery — CoreDNS, resolv.conf, kube-proxy modes (iptables/ipvs)
Category 5: Cluster Architecture (25% — Q151-Q200)
  • Q151-Q165: Cluster setup — kubeadm init/join/upgrade, node management, static pods
  • Q166-Q175: etcd — backup/restore, snapshot, compaction, defrag, RAFT consensus
  • Q176-Q185: RBAC — ServiceAccount, Role, ClusterRole, RoleBinding, certificate signing
  • Q186-Q200: Architecture — API server, scheduler, controller-manager, container runtimes, kubelet config

⚡ Appendix M: CKA Exam Day — 20 Command Patterns You MUST KnowCRAM SHEET

🏆 Master Summary — How to Use These 20 Patterns

This appendix is your last-30-minutes-before-exam cram sheet. Every pattern here has appeared on real CKA exams. They are organized by domain weight — start with Troubleshooting (30%) and Cluster Architecture (25%) patterns first, as they make up 55% of your score.

📖
Step 1: Read all 20 patterns once (15 min)
✍️
Step 2: Type M.1, M.2, M.6, M.10 from memory (10 min)
✅
Step 3: Verify each command & take the exam (5 min buffer)
🎯 Implementation Strategy — How Each Pattern Maps to Exam Tasks
  • M.1–M.2 (etcd + kubeadm upgrade): These are guaranteed 2 tasks. etcd backup/restore is worth heavy points. kubeadm upgrade sequence must be exact. Type these from memory 5 times before exam.
  • M.3, M.13, M.17 (RBAC + SA + CSR): Security tasks appear in every CKA exam. Know RBAC imperatives cold. ServiceAccount + RoleBinding is a common combo task.
  • M.4, M.5, M.19 (Debug + Logs + Monitoring): Troubleshooting is 30% of your score. The debug pod pattern (kubectl run --rm -it) and log flags (--tail --since --previous) are your primary diagnostic tools.
  • M.6, M.10 (Imperative YAML + Rollouts): Your #1 speed hack. Generate YAML with --dry-run=client -o yaml instead of typing 20-line manifests. Saves 30+ minutes across the exam.
  • M.7, M.11 (Node maintenance + Static pods): Cordon → drain → work → uncordon is tested on most exams. Static pod manifests in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ — remember: delete the FILE, not the pod.
  • M.8, M.17 (TLS + Certificates): TLS Ingress with kubernetes.io/tls Secret type. Certificate approval for joining nodes. Know: kubeadm certs check-expiration and kubectl certificate approve.
  • M.12, M.14 (NetworkPolicy + CronJob): NetworkPolicy is a frequent task — create a policy targeting specific pods with correct Ingress/Egress direction. CronJob: 5-field syntax, suspend/resume, manual trigger.
  • M.15, M.16, M.18, M.20 (Safe apply + JSONPath + Context + etcdctl): These are your "finishing touches" patterns. kubectl diff before applying. JSONPath for extracting specific fields. Context switching to avoid managing the wrong cluster. etcdctl deep dive for troubleshooting.

These are the 20 command patterns that appear on EVERY CKA exam. Memorize them cold. Practice typing them from memory — the exam has no autocomplete beyond kubectl built-in completion. Read this appendix in the 30 minutes before your exam.

M.1 — etcd Backup & Restore (Full TLS Flags)
1

Backup:

ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
  --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
  --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  snapshot save /opt/etcd-backup.db

Restore:

ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restore \
  snapshot restore /opt/etcd-backup.db

⚠️ After restore: Update etcd static pod manifest (/etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml) to point --data-dir to the new path, then restart kubelet. Also update the --initial-cluster-token to a new value.

M.2 — kubeadm Cluster Upgrade Sequence
2

On Control Plane:

# 1. Unhold and upgrade kubeadm

apt-mark unhold kubeadm && apt-get update && apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.30.x-*
apt-mark hold kubeadm
# 2. Verify plan
kubeadm upgrade plan
# 3. Apply upgrade
kubeadm upgrade apply v1.30.x
# 4. Upgrade kubelet & kubectl
apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl && apt-get install -y kubelet=1.30.x-* kubectl=1.30.x-*
apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl
systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart kubelet

On Each Worker:

# 1. Drain the worker first (from CP)

kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
# 2. SSH to worker, upgrade kubeadm + kubelet
apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.30.x-* kubelet=1.30.x-*
kubeadm upgrade node
systemctl restart kubelet
# 3. Uncordon
kubectl uncordon worker-1
M.3 — RBAC: auth can-i, Impersonation & Dry-Run
3
# Test if user can do something

kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=alice -n dev
kubectl auth can-i delete secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:dev:api-sa -n dev

# List ALL permissions for a user
kubectl auth can-i --list --as=alice -n dev

# Create RBAC imperatively (FAST — use on exam!)
kubectl create role pod-reader --verb=get,list,watch --resource=pods -n dev
kubectl create rolebinding alice-pod --role=pod-reader --user=alice -n dev
kubectl create clusterrole secret-reader --verb=get,list --resource=secrets
kubectl create clusterrolebinding alice-secret --clusterrole=secret-reader --user=alice

# For ServiceAccounts
kubectl create rolebinding sa-binding --role=pod-reader --serviceaccount=dev:api-sa -n dev
M.4 — Debug Pod & Network Testing
4
# Temporary debug pod (auto-deletes on exit)

kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox --restart=Never -- sh

# Test DNS from debug pod
kubectl exec deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj -- nslookup kubernetes.default
kubectl exec deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj -- nslookup -type=SRV _port._tcp.svc.ns

# Test connectivity
kubectl run curl-test --rm -it --image=curlimages/curl --restart=Never -- \
  curl -v http://svc-name.ns.svc.cluster.local:8080

# Port forward for local debugging
kubectl port-forward svc/anihpj-api 8080:80 -n anihpj
kubectl port-forward pod/anihpj-api-7d8f9 5432:5432 -n anihpj

# Copy files to/from pods
kubectl cp anihpj/config.yaml pod/name:/etc/config.yaml -n anihpj
kubectl cp pod/name:/var/log/app.log ./app.log -n anihpj
M.5 — Logs: --tail, --since, --timestamps, --previous
5
# Logs from a specific container in a multi-container pod

kubectl logs pod/name -c sidecar -n anihpj

# Last N lines
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --tail=50 -n anihpj

# Logs since a relative time
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --since=10m -n anihpj
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --since=1h -n anihpj

# Logs with timestamps
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --timestamps -n anihpj

# Logs from PREVIOUS (crashed) container
kubectl logs pod/name --previous -n anihpj

# Follow logs in real-time
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --follow -n anihpj

# Combined: last 100 lines from previous container with timestamps
kubectl logs pod/name --previous --tail=100 --timestamps -n anihpj
🏆 EXAM DAY SPEED TIPS
  • Alias kubectl: alias k=kubectl; complete -F __start_kubectl k — saves 30+ keystrokes per command
  • Use imperative + dry-run: Generate YAML in 2 seconds instead of writing 20 lines from scratch
  • Verify context FIRST: Every task starts with kubectl config use-context <given-context>
  • Use kubectl explain: Forget a field? kubectl explain pod.spec --recursive
  • Copy-paste carefully: The exam terminal supports copy-paste but verify after pasting
  • Flag order matters: kubectl logs pod-name -n ns --tail=10 NOT kubectl logs --tail=10 pod-name
  • Weighted scoring: Troubleshooting (30%) > Cluster Architecture (25%) = 55% of your score. Prioritize these!
  • Leave no task blank: Partial solutions get partial credit. Even creating the namespace and YAML file earns points.
